English for Work · Customers & creative work

Restaurant Serving English

Take precise table orders, explain menu choices, coordinate courses, handle dining concerns, and resolve bill questions in natural spoken English.

8 practical lessonsIntermediate to advanced1 complete learner book
Start practicing →

Words into action

Read the situation.
Find your words.
Make yourself clear.

For Restaurant servers, food runners, and dining-room service staff.

Open learner book (PDF) ↗

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Attach each dish and modifier to the correct guest, then read back the order before sending it.
  • Explain a supplement, calculate comparable menu prices, and leave the purchase choice with the guest.
  • Receive a reported allergy, ask the kitchen a precise question, and communicate unresolved ingredient and preparation information.
  • Explain a kitchen estimate against a guest departure time without converting readiness into a guaranteed meal completion.
  • Acknowledge an order mismatch, offer a specific remedy, and separate replacement timing from a bill-adjustment decision.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All restaurants, prices, guests, and service events are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not food-safety certification, medical advice, or payment compliance. Follow current local law and actual allergy, food-handling, payment, and emergency procedures. Menu descriptions do not establish allergen safety. Do not delay urgent help for a routine service conversation.

01Confirming seats and order detailsWho asked for no onions?+

Your goal

Attach each dish and modifier to the correct guest, then read back the order before sending it.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Who asked for no onions?

At fictional table 12, three guests are ordering. Seat one wants mushroom pasta; seat two wants salad with dressing separately; seat three has not chosen. Someone says "no onions," but server Lena cannot tell which dish the request belongs to. She has not sent the order. Guest Sam at seat two confirms that the onion request belongs to the salad. The dialogue follows Lena and section lead Cam checking that clarified request before transmission. Seat three still needs time. No allergy is reported in this case, and the onion request must not be treated as a confirmed allergy or a rule for every dish.

Lena: Server / Cam: Section lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which seat has not chosen?

Answer and explanation

C. Seat three

The brief explicitly leaves seat three's dish undecided.

2. Which request already belongs to the salad?

Answer and explanation

B. Dressing separately

Seat two requested salad with the dressing served separately.

3. What did Lena need to clarify when she first heard the request?

Answer and explanation

D. Which dish should have no onions

The onion request initially lacked a dish reference; Sam subsequently attached it to the salad.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

No onions needs a clear dish reference. Do not attach it to every plate because the speaker was unclear. A modifier describes the requested change; a reported allergy requires its own accurate communication and the actual allergy process.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
cover
One diner counted for restaurant service or planning.serve three covers
seat number
Identifier linking an order to a place at a table.confirm the seat number
table number
Identifier used to locate a dining table.check the table number
order pad
Written surface used to record guest orders.update the order pad
point of sale
System for entering orders and recording sales, often abbreviated POS.enter the order at the point of sale
order ticket
Record of dishes and instructions sent for preparation.read the order ticket
modifier
Instruction changing an item from its standard preparation or presentation.attach a modifier
omission
Requested removal of an ingredient or component.confirm the omission
substitution
Replacement of one component with another when available and permitted.check a substitution
on the side
Served separately rather than mixed into or placed on the dish.serve dressing on the side
read-back
Spoken repetition of recorded details to check accuracy.give a read-back
seat reference
Link showing which guest a detail concerns.clarify the seat reference
course
Stage of a meal, such as a starter or main.enter the main course
main
Principal dish in a meal.choose a main
starter
Dish served before the main course in the relevant menu format.order a starter
garnish
Item added to finish or accompany a dish.identify the garnish
accompaniment
Food served alongside a principal item.describe the accompaniment
preparation request
Guest instruction about how an item should be prepared.confirm the preparation request
dietary requirement
Food-related need that must be described accurately and handled appropriately.clarify a dietary requirement
preference
Stated choice that must not automatically be labeled a medical condition.record a preference
pending choice
Selection that has not yet been made.keep a choice pending
send the order
Transmit a confirmed order through the restaurant's system.send the order
order sequence
Agreed order in which information or dishes are handled.preserve the order sequence
confirmation
Explicit agreement that repeated details are correct.obtain confirmation

Seat three has not chosen is not the same as seat three is not eating. Confirmed items and pending choices can coexist. Follow the restaurant process for sending partial orders, but never invent a third dish to complete a ticket.

C Notice the language

Clarification is part of attention

A guest may speak while another person is ordering, or answer on behalf of someone else. A brief, neutral check avoids blame and keeps the exchange moving. Say which detail needs clarification rather than asking the whole table to repeat everything.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify the dish
Which dish should have no onions?
Check the guest
Is that request for your salad?
Read back seat one
Seat one is the mushroom pasta.
Read back seat two
Seat two is the salad with dressing on the side.
Preserve the pending choice
Seat three is still deciding.
Attach the modifier
I have noted no onions for your salad.
Avoid a table-wide change
I will not apply that request to the other dishes.
Ask briefly
May I check one detail before I send this?
Translate separately
You would like the dressing served separately, correct?
Separate request and guarantee
I will confirm any preparation question through our kitchen process.
Keep the record aligned
The seat reference and modifier need to stay together.
Avoid an invented allergy
No allergy has been reported in this exchange.
Confirm understanding
Have I matched the dishes and requests correctly?
Allow time
I will give seat three another moment to choose.
Keep transmission accurate
The order has not been sent yet.
Close with the corrected detail
Salad for seat two, no onions, dressing on the side.
6 language and precision notes
Which dish
Targets the missing reference instead of questioning the guest's reason.
Still deciding
Describes an unfinished choice without canceling the meal.
On the side
Common serving phrase meaning separately.
Put on the request
Refers to recording the instruction, not adding onions to the food.
Yet
Distinguishes an unsent order from one already in preparation.
Matched correctly
Invites correction before an avoidable error moves to the kitchen.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which clarification is most useful?

Answer and explanation

A. Which dish should have no onions?

The question asks for the missing dish reference without blame or unsupported changes.

2. What should be sent as the salad modifier after Sam confirms?

Answer and explanation

D. No onions, dressing on the side

Sam confirms the onion request for the salad, which already has separate dressing.

3. Which statement preserves the pending choice?

Answer and explanation

D. Seat three is still deciding.

The brief leaves the third choice open rather than establishing a dish or cancellation.

4. What does the onion request establish medically?

Answer and explanation

A. No allergy diagnosis or report by itself

A requested omission alone does not supply an allergy report or a safety conclusion.

E Conversations

Who asked for no onions?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Lena: Server / Cam: Section lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • modifier
  • omission
  • confirmation
  • pending choice
  • order ticket
  • covers
  • seat reference
  • read-back
  • send the order
  • on the side
  1. Lena
    Cam, can you check table twelve with me before I send it? Three guests, two mains chosen. I clarified the onion request with Sam at seat two.
  2. Cam
    Yes. Which dish gets the 1? Your first note says no onions between the two mains, so I cannot tell which one it follows.
  3. Lena
    It belongs to the salad, not the pasta. Sam also wants the dressing separately. I have corrected the note against seat two.
  4. Cam
    Salad, no onions, dressing 2. That is clear. What is seat one having?
  5. Lena
    Mushroom pasta, unchanged. Seat three was still looking at the menu when I came over, so I have left that choice open.
  6. Cam
    Keep it as a 3, then. Do not copy the pasta into the third seat just to fill the space.
  7. Lena
    I will go back to that guest. Before I do, can you repeat what you have? I want to catch any mismatch now.
  8. Cam
    Here is the 4: table twelve, seat one mushroom pasta; seat two salad, no onions, dressing on the side; seat three still deciding.
  9. Lena
    Correct. The salad has both requests. I originally wrote them between the items because two people were speaking at once.
  10. Cam
    Now the 5 makes the connection clear. Keep the requests attached to that item in the system too.
  11. Lena
    I have not sent anything yet. I wanted to settle the unclear detail first rather than ask the kitchen to interpret my note.
  12. Cam
    Good. The 6 needs the same dish-and-seat links. A general no-onions note across the table would give the kitchen a different instruction.
  13. Lena
    The screen shows three diners, but only two main dishes so far. That is why the totals do not match yet.
  14. Cam
    Three 7, two confirmed mains. That is fine while the last person is choosing. The diner count does not supply a missing order.
  15. Lena
    Sam said no onions, but did not report an allergy. I repeated the request and confirmed the dish rather than guessing the reason.
  16. Cam
    Record the 8 accurately. If an allergy is reported, use our allergy procedure; do not treat an ordinary modifier as a substitute for that process.
  17. Lena
    I will return to seat three now and ask what they would like. Then I can repeat the complete order to the table.
  18. Cam
    Get that last 9 from the guest. Leave Sam's salad request as it is unless Sam changes it.
  19. Lena
    Understood: pasta unchanged, salad with its two requests, third main still open. I will come back if the guest needs a kitchen check.
  20. Cam
    All right. Follow our process when you 10. Keep the dish, seat, and instructions together so the kitchen receives what the guests actually asked for.

Answers and explanations
  1. modifier Turn 2. The modifier changes the salad order; Lena has confirmed that the onion request belongs to Sam at seat two.
  2. on the side Turn 4. On the side means the dressing is served separately, as Sam requested for the salad.
  3. pending choice Turn 6. Seat three has not selected a dish; a pending choice is neither a cancellation nor an order.
  4. read-back Turn 8. A read-back repeats the recorded details so the colleagues can catch an error before transmission.
  5. seat reference Turn 10. The seat reference attaches both salad requests to seat two and leaves seat one's pasta unchanged.
  6. order ticket Turn 12. The order ticket communicates preparation instructions; its modifiers must stay with the correct dish when sent.
  7. covers Turn 14. Covers counts diners; three diners does not establish three completed main-course orders.
  8. omission Turn 16. The omission is removal of onions from the salad; no allergy has been reported in this case.
  9. confirmation Turn 18. Confirmation checks the actual chosen dish and details; the unfinished third choice cannot be confirmed in advance.
  10. send the order Turn 20. Send the order means transmit the checked details, after resolving the remaining choice through the restaurant's process.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Lena: Cam, can you check table twelve with me before I send it? Three guests, two mains chosen. I clarified the onion request with Sam at seat two.
  2. Cam: Yes. Which dish gets the modifier? Your first note says no onions between the two mains, so I cannot tell which one it follows.
  3. Lena: It belongs to the salad, not the pasta. Sam also wants the dressing separately. I have corrected the note against seat two.
  4. Cam: Salad, no onions, dressing on the side. That is clear. What is seat one having?
  5. Lena: Mushroom pasta, unchanged. Seat three was still looking at the menu when I came over, so I have left that choice open.
  6. Cam: Keep it as a pending choice, then. Do not copy the pasta into the third seat just to fill the space.
  7. Lena: I will go back to that guest. Before I do, can you repeat what you have? I want to catch any mismatch now.
  8. Cam: Here is the read-back: table twelve, seat one mushroom pasta; seat two salad, no onions, dressing on the side; seat three still deciding.
  9. Lena: Correct. The salad has both requests. I originally wrote them between the items because two people were speaking at once.
  10. Cam: Now the seat reference makes the connection clear. Keep the requests attached to that item in the system too.
  11. Lena: I have not sent anything yet. I wanted to settle the unclear detail first rather than ask the kitchen to interpret my note.
  12. Cam: Good. The order ticket needs the same dish-and-seat links. A general no-onions note across the table would give the kitchen a different instruction.
  13. Lena: The screen shows three diners, but only two main dishes so far. That is why the totals do not match yet.
  14. Cam: Three covers, two confirmed mains. That is fine while the last person is choosing. The diner count does not supply a missing order.
  15. Lena: Sam said no onions, but did not report an allergy. I repeated the request and confirmed the dish rather than guessing the reason.
  16. Cam: Record the omission accurately. If an allergy is reported, use our allergy procedure; do not treat an ordinary modifier as a substitute for that process.
  17. Lena: I will return to seat three now and ask what they would like. Then I can repeat the complete order to the table.
  18. Cam: Get that last confirmation from the guest. Leave Sam's salad request as it is unless Sam changes it.
  19. Lena: Understood: pasta unchanged, salad with its two requests, third main still open. I will come back if the guest needs a kitchen check.
  20. Cam: All right. Follow our process when you send the order. Keep the dish, seat, and instructions together so the kitchen receives what the guests actually asked for.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Whose onion request?

Table 12: the three-cover order in the case.

  1. Server: I caught the request for no onions, but which dish does that apply to?
  2. Guest: My salad, please. My friend wants the mushroom pasta exactly as it normally comes.
  3. Server: Thank you. Are you also the person who asked for dressing on the side?
  4. Guest: Yes, I want the dressing separately, not removed from the order altogether, please.
  5. Server: Then seat two has the salad, no onions, with dressing in a separate serving.
  6. Guest: That's right. The person beside the window hasn't chosen a main course yet.
  7. Server: I have seat one down for pasta and seat three as still deciding.
  8. Guest: Could you give seat three another minute before you send the main course order?
  9. Server: Certainly. I'll wait here a moment; would another menu help with the choice?
  10. Guest: Yes, please. The third menu is underneath our coats, and another would be useful.
Moving chairs after ordering

Two diners have swapped seats before a runner arrives.

  1. Runner: I have the vegetable tart for seat one. Did anyone change seats after ordering?
  2. Guest: Yes, we swapped so my colleague could sit farther away from the speaker.
  3. Runner: Thanks for telling me. Who ordered the tart with the small green salad?
  4. Guest: I did, and I'm now sitting beside the aisle rather than the window.
  5. Runner: Then this plate belongs here. The pasta is for your colleague by the window?
  6. Guest: Correct. Neither of us changed the dishes; we only moved to different chairs.
  7. Runner: I'll tell your server about the move so the dessert orders reach you correctly.
  8. Guest: Please do. We're ordering different desserts and would rather not switch plates again.
  9. Runner: I'll identify the dishes as I bring them, as well as checking the updated seats.
  10. Guest: That works. You can put the tart here, with the salad beside it.
A phrase lost in the noise

A server checks an incomplete order during a busy lunch.

  1. Server: I'm sorry, the music covered your last words. Was that soup and a sandwich?
  2. Guest: Soup instead of a sandwich, actually. I don't need both items for lunch.
  3. Server: Thank you for correcting that. Which soup would you like from today's two choices?
  4. Guest: The tomato soup, please, with the bread that appears beside it on the menu.
  5. Server: I have one tomato soup with bread. Would you like the small or large portion?
  6. Guest: The small one. I only have time for a light lunch before my meeting.
  7. Server: Let me read that back: one small tomato soup with bread, and no sandwich.
  8. Guest: Exactly. Could you also check whether my colleague's drink has already been ordered?
  9. Server: Your colleague asked me for sparkling water earlier, and that order is already in.
  10. Guest: Great. Then the soup is the only item you still need to add.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Who asked for no onions?
  1. Check the cloze answers. Read Lena and Cam's exchange, stressing table twelve, seat one, seat two, and seat three.
  2. Switch roles. Say the final read-back without changing the pasta or inventing the third main.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Attach a different request

Table 8 has two covers. Seat one orders tomato soup; seat two orders grilled fish. A lemon-separately request is clarified as belonging to the fish. No third diner or allergy is reported.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Server: "The lemon request belongs to the ___."

Answer and explanation

D. fish

The clarification specifically attaches the separate lemon to the grilled fish.

2. Guest: "That is seat ___."

Answer and explanation

D. two

The guest ordering grilled fish is in seat two.

3. Server: "The tomato soup remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unchanged

No modification to the soup is supplied in the facts.

4. Guest: "There are two ___."

Answer and explanation

D. covers

Covers counts diners, and this table has two diners.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Confirming seats and order details
Original fictional case: At fictional table 12, three guests are ordering. Seat one wants mushroom pasta; seat two wants salad with dressing separately; seat three has not chosen. Someone says "no onions," but server Lena cannot tell which dish the request belongs to. She has not sent the order. Guest Sam at seat two confirms that the onion request belongs to the salad. The dialogue follows Lena and section lead Cam checking that clarified request before transmission. Seat three still needs time. No allergy is reported in this case, and the onion request must not be treated as a confirmed allergy or a rule for every dish.
Language workshop: Who asked for no onions?
Communication goal: Attach each dish and modifier to the correct guest, then read back the order before sending it.
Language guidance: No onions needs a clear dish reference. Do not attach it to every plate because the speaker was unclear. A modifier describes the requested change; a reported allergy requires its own accurate communication and the actual allergy process.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which dish should have no onions? / Is that request for your salad? / Seat one is the mushroom pasta. / Seat two is the salad with dressing on the side. / Seat three is still deciding. / I have noted no onions for your salad. / I will not apply that request to the other dishes. / May I check one detail before I send this? / You would like the dressing served separately, correct? / I will confirm any preparation question through our kitchen process. / The seat reference and modifier need to stay together. / No allergy has been reported in this exchange. / Have I matched the dishes and requests correctly? / I will give seat three another moment to choose. / The order has not been sent yet. / Salad for seat two, no onions, dressing on the side.
Vocabulary: cover: One diner counted for restaurant service or planning. / seat number: Identifier linking an order to a place at a table. / table number: Identifier used to locate a dining table. / order pad: Written surface used to record guest orders. / point of sale: System for entering orders and recording sales, often abbreviated POS. / order ticket: Record of dishes and instructions sent for preparation. / modifier: Instruction changing an item from its standard preparation or presentation. / omission: Requested removal of an ingredient or component. / substitution: Replacement of one component with another when available and permitted. / on the side: Served separately rather than mixed into or placed on the dish. / read-back: Spoken repetition of recorded details to check accuracy. / seat reference: Link showing which guest a detail concerns. / course: Stage of a meal, such as a starter or main. / main: Principal dish in a meal. / starter: Dish served before the main course in the relevant menu format. / garnish: Item added to finish or accompany a dish. / accompaniment: Food served alongside a principal item. / preparation request: Guest instruction about how an item should be prepared. / dietary requirement: Food-related need that must be described accurately and handled appropriately. / preference: Stated choice that must not automatically be labeled a medical condition. / pending choice: Selection that has not yet been made. / send the order: Transmit a confirmed order through the restaurant's system. / order sequence: Agreed order in which information or dishes are handled. / confirmation: Explicit agreement that repeated details are correct.
Speaking task: Check the cloze answers. Read Lena and Cam's exchange, stressing table twelve, seat one, seat two, and seat three. Switch roles. Say the final read-back without changing the pasta or inventing the third main. Complete the table 8 transfer and check its key. Repeat with a partner: fish, seat two, soup unchanged, two covers.
Partner: Lena: Server / Cam: Section lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Table 8 has two covers. Seat one orders tomato soup; seat two orders grilled fish. A lemon-separately request is clarified as belonging to the fish. No third diner or allergy is reported.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Lena: I will return to seat three now and ask what they would like. Then I can repeat the complete order to the table.
Cam: Get that last confirmation from the guest. Leave Sam's salad request as it is unless Sam changes it.
Lena: Understood: pasta unchanged, salad with its two requests, third main still open. I will come back if the guest needs a kitchen check.
Cam: All right. Follow our process when you send the order. Keep the dish, seat, and instructions together so the kitchen receives what the guests actually asked for.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Communication goal: Attach each dish and modifier to the correct guest, then read back the order before sending it.
Language guidance: No onions needs a clear dish reference. Do not attach it to every plate because the speaker was unclear. A modifier describes the requested change; a reported allergy requires its own accurate communication and the actual allergy process.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which dish should have no onions? / Is that request for your salad? / Seat one is the mushroom pasta. / Seat two is the salad with dressing on the side. / Seat three is still deciding. / I have noted no onions for your salad. / I will not apply that request to the other dishes. / May I check one detail before I send this? / You would like the dressing served separately, correct? / I will confirm any preparation question through our kitchen process. / The seat reference and modifier need to stay together. / No allergy has been reported in this exchange. / Have I matched the dishes and requests correctly? / I will give seat three another moment to choose. / The order has not been sent yet. / Salad for seat two, no onions, dressing on the side.
Vocabulary: cover: One diner counted for restaurant service or planning. / seat number: Identifier linking an order to a place at a table. / table number: Identifier used to locate a dining table. / order pad: Written surface used to record guest orders. / point of sale: System for entering orders and recording sales, often abbreviated POS. / order ticket: Record of dishes and instructions sent for preparation. / modifier: Instruction changing an item from its standard preparation or presentation. / omission: Requested removal of an ingredient or component. / substitution: Replacement of one component with another when available and permitted. / on the side: Served separately rather than mixed into or placed on the dish. / read-back: Spoken repetition of recorded details to check accuracy. / seat reference: Link showing which guest a detail concerns. / course: Stage of a meal, such as a starter or main. / main: Principal dish in a meal. / starter: Dish served before the main course in the relevant menu format. / garnish: Item added to finish or accompany a dish. / accompaniment: Food served alongside a principal item. / preparation request: Guest instruction about how an item should be prepared. / dietary requirement: Food-related need that must be described accurately and handled appropriately. / preference: Stated choice that must not automatically be labeled a medical condition. / pending choice: Selection that has not yet been made. / send the order: Transmit a confirmed order through the restaurant's system. / order sequence: Agreed order in which information or dishes are handled. / confirmation: Explicit agreement that repeated details are correct.
Speaking task: Check the cloze answers. Read Lena and Cam's exchange, stressing table twelve, seat one, seat two, and seat three. Switch roles. Say the final read-back without changing the pasta or inventing the third main. Complete the table 8 transfer and check its key. Repeat with a partner: fish, seat two, soup unchanged, two covers.
Partner: Lena: Server / Cam: Section lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Table 8 has two covers. Seat one orders tomato soup; seat two orders grilled fish. A lemon-separately request is clarified as belonging to the fish. No third diner or allergy is reported.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Lena: I will return to seat three now and ask what they would like. Then I can repeat the complete order to the table.
Cam: Get that last confirmation from the guest. Leave Sam's salad request as it is unless Sam changes it.
Lena: Understood: pasta unchanged, salad with its two requests, third main still open. I will come back if the guest needs a kitchen check.
Cam: All right. Follow our process when you send the order. Keep the dish, seat, and instructions together so the kitchen receives what the guests actually asked for.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

02Explaining menu choices and pricesThe steak set or steak alone?+

Your goal

Explain a supplement, calculate comparable menu prices, and leave the purchase choice with the guest.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

The steak set or steak alone?

The fictional lunch set is $24 and includes a main and dessert. Choosing steak adds a $5 supplement to that set price. The a la carte steak is $27 without dessert. Both steak options include potatoes. Guest Jordan initially thinks the steak set must cost $24, then wonders whether potatoes cost extra. Server Priya must compare the $29 steak set with the $27 steak alone, explain the two-dollar difference, and avoid inventing a separate dessert price, drink inclusion, or tax policy. No choice has been made at the start.

Jordan: Lunch guest / Priya: Server

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the steak set price?

Answer and explanation

D. $29

The $24 base plus the $5 steak supplement equals $29.

2. Which item is included in both steak options?

Answer and explanation

B. Potatoes

Both the steak set and a la carte steak include potatoes.

3. What is not supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. A separate dessert price

The case gives the set comparison, not a standalone dessert price.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The $5 supplement belongs to the $24 set base: $24 + $5 = $29. It is not added to the $27 a la carte price. The comparison is $29 with dessert versus $27 without dessert, with potatoes in both.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
set menu
Group of included courses offered for a stated price, subject to listed conditions.explain the set menu
a la carte
Ordered as individual menu items rather than as a set.order a la carte
supplement
Additional charge added to a stated base price.apply the steak supplement
base price
Starting price before specified additions.state the base price
inclusive price
Price covering the items or charges explicitly named as included.explain the inclusive price
inclusion
Item or service covered by the quoted option.compare the inclusions
exclusion
Item or charge not covered by an option.clarify an exclusion
price difference
Amount separating two prices on a comparable basis.calculate the price difference
standalone price
Price charged for an item bought separately.verify the standalone price
upgrade
Change to a different or enhanced option, often at an added cost.explain the upgrade
main-and-dessert set
Meal option combining a main course and dessert.choose the main-and-dessert set
side dish
Food served alongside a principal dish.include a side dish
portion
Amount served to one person or as specified.describe the portion
menu description
Written account of a dish or offer.read the menu description
daily special
Dish or offer identified as special for the relevant service period.explain the daily special
availability
Whether an item can currently be ordered.check availability
value comparison
Assessment of price alongside the contents or benefits of an option.make a value comparison
additional charge
Amount payable beyond a specified quoted price.identify an additional charge
price clarification
Explanation resolving uncertainty about what an amount means.give a price clarification
itemized total
Sum showing the individual components that make up the charge.present an itemized total
optional extra
Additional item that is not compulsory or automatically included.describe an optional extra
menu pairing
Combination of items offered or suggested together.explain the menu pairing
price basis
Items and conditions to which a quoted price applies.state the price basis
guest choice
Selection made by the diner after receiving relevant information.confirm the guest choice

A two-dollar difference between these options does not establish that every dessert costs two dollars separately. Do not promise identical portions, included drinks, or an all-in bill total when those details are absent. Explain the menu prices actually supplied.

C Notice the language

Help the guest compare

A recommendation should explain a relevant difference without pressuring the guest or implying a more expensive option is always better. State the total for each option and its inclusions. Let the guest decide whether the added item is worth the difference.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Introduce the set
The lunch set includes a main and dessert.
State the base
The set starts at $24.
Explain the addition
Steak carries a $5 supplement on that set.
Give the combined price
That makes the steak set $29.
Introduce the alternative
The a la carte steak is $27.
Name the difference in contents
The a la carte option does not include dessert.
Keep shared inclusions clear
Both steak options include potatoes.
Calculate the difference
The set is two dollars more than steak alone.
Avoid an unsupported standalone price
That difference is not a quoted separate dessert price.
Respond to a price misunderstanding
The $24 is the base, before the steak supplement.
Do not invent a surcharge
No extra potato charge is listed in these options.
Keep the choice open
Would you prefer the set with dessert or the steak alone?
Avoid pressure
It depends on whether you want the included dessert.
Separate unknown details
I would need to check any detail not stated on this menu.
Confirm the chosen option
Let me confirm which option you would like.
Summarize evenly
$29 with dessert, or $27 without dessert; potatoes are included in both.
6 language and precision notes
Starts at
Signals a base price that may change with a specified selection.
Carries a supplement
Common menu language for an added charge.
That makes
Introduces the result of a transparent calculation.
Both
Prevents a shared inclusion from sounding exclusive to one option.
Two dollars more
Compares these options without assigning every component a standalone price.
Would you prefer
Gives the decision to the guest after the comparison.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which calculation is correct?

Answer and explanation

B. $24 + $5 = $29 for the steak set

The supplement is added to the set base, not to the a la carte price.

2. Which comparison is complete?

Answer and explanation

D. $29 with dessert versus $27 without dessert; both include potatoes.

This comparison gives the actual prices and distinguishes shared and different inclusions.

3. What cannot be inferred from the two-dollar difference?

Answer and explanation

B. The restaurant's standalone dessert price is $2.

A difference between bundled offers does not establish the separate selling price of a component.

4. Which recommendation avoids pressure?

Answer and explanation

B. If you want dessert, the set includes it for two dollars more than steak alone.

The recommendation uses the supplied price and inclusion difference without inventing benefits or removing choice.

E Conversations

The steak set or steak alone?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Jordan: Lunch guest / Priya: Server

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • supplement
  • inclusion
  • main-and-dessert set
  • base price
  • price difference

  • a la carte

  • guest choice
  • value comparison
  1. Jordan
    Could you help me with the lunch menu? I saw twenty-four dollars beside the set, but there is another amount beside the steak.
  2. Priya
    Certainly. Twenty-four dollars is the 1 for a main and dessert. Choosing steak adds five dollars, so that particular set comes to twenty-nine.
  3. Jordan
    So the steak is five dollars extra on the set? I first read that as a separate price, which did not seem right.
  4. Priya
    Exactly. It is a 2 to the set. Twenty-four plus five is twenty-nine for the steak set, including dessert.
  5. Jordan
    I also see steak listed separately at twenty-seven. Is that another way of ordering the same main without taking the lunch set?
  6. Priya
    Yes, that is the 3 option. The steak is twenty-seven without dessert. Both options include potatoes, so you do not need to add them to this comparison.
  7. Jordan
    Good, I was about to ask about the potatoes. I do not want to discover I need to order a side as well.
  8. Priya
    Potatoes are a shared 4 here. The difference stated on this menu is the dessert, alongside the two different menu prices.
  9. Jordan
    Then the set is two dollars more than the steak alone. I am not especially hungry, so I am deciding whether I want dessert.
  10. Priya
    That is the correct 5. If you want the included dessert, the set is twenty-nine. If not, the steak alone is twenty-seven.
  11. Jordan
    Could I order the steak alone and add dessert for two dollars later, or does that price only work with the set?
  12. Priya
    I would need to check the 6 before quoting that. The two-dollar comparison does not mean every separately ordered dessert has a two-dollar price.
  13. Jordan
    That makes sense. I may not want dessert after the steak. Could you give me a moment to choose?
  14. Priya
    Of course. A useful 7 depends on what you want to eat. The more expensive option is not automatically the better choice for you.
  15. Jordan
    Is coffee included with the dessert in that set? I sometimes see that elsewhere, but I cannot find it in this description.
  16. Priya
    Coffee is not part of the stated 8 in the details we are comparing. I would check its price and status separately rather than tell you it is part of the set.
  17. Jordan
    Thanks. I will leave coffee for now. Could you repeat just the two steak prices for me?
  18. Priya
    Then the 9 is twenty-nine for steak, potatoes, and the included dessert, or twenty-seven for steak and potatoes without dessert.
  19. Jordan
    Let me check with my friend about dessert before I decide. I have not chosen the set yet.
  20. Priya
    Certainly. I will wait for your 10. When you are ready, tell me set or a la carte, and I will confirm the option before entering it.

Answers and explanations
  1. base price Turn 2. The base price is the set's starting amount before the listed five-dollar steak supplement is added.
  2. supplement Turn 4. A supplement is an addition to the stated base, not a replacement price or the cost of the whole dish.
  3. a la carte Turn 6. A la carte identifies an individual menu item rather than the main-and-dessert set being compared.
  4. inclusion Turn 8. An inclusion is covered by the quoted option; potatoes are included in both steak choices in this menu.
  5. price difference Turn 10. The price difference is twenty-nine minus twenty-seven, or two dollars between these two specific options.
  6. standalone price Turn 12. The standalone price applies to a dessert bought separately; the two-dollar bundle difference does not establish it.
  7. value comparison Turn 14. A value comparison considers both price and inclusions, allowing the guest to decide whether the dessert matters.
  8. main-and-dessert set Turn 16. The main-and-dessert set names the included courses; coffee is not supplied as an inclusion in this comparison.
  9. price basis Turn 18. The price basis specifies what each quoted amount covers, avoiding unsupported claims about other items or charges.
  10. guest choice Turn 20. The guest choice must be confirmed explicitly; discussing an option at length does not authorize ordering it.
Read the complete conversation

Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The five-dollar misunderstanding

The lunch menu in the case is open between server and guest.

  1. Guest: The steak says five dollars underneath it. Is that really the price of the meal?
  2. Server: That's the supplement to the twenty-four-dollar set menu, making the steak set twenty-nine dollars.
  3. Guest: Does that include the potatoes, or would those be another item on my bill?
  4. Server: Potatoes are included. The set also includes one dessert from the choices listed below.
  5. Guest: I don't usually have dessert. Is there a price for just the steak and potatoes?
  6. Server: Yes, the a la carte steak is twenty-seven dollars, with potatoes but without dessert.
  7. Guest: Then the extra two dollars on the set gets me one of those desserts?
  8. Server: For these two options, yes. The difference is two dollars and the included dessert.
  9. Guest: I'll choose the separate steak. Please don't add the set menu to my order.
  10. Server: Certainly. One a la carte steak with potatoes, priced at twenty-seven dollars before any applicable charges.
A starter is not a side

A diner compares a side salad with a larger starter salad.

  1. Guest: Could I have the starter salad with my main instead of the little side salad?
  2. Server: You can order the starter salad separately. It's larger and has its own menu price.
  3. Guest: I thought every salad was included when the main says it comes with a salad.
  4. Server: That refers to the side salad listed with the main, not the starter selection above.
  5. Guest: How much more would the starter cost, and could you remove the included side?
  6. Server: The starter is eight dollars. Removing the included side doesn't reduce this main's listed price.
  7. Guest: In that case, I'll keep the included salad and skip the larger starter today.
  8. Server: Of course. Would you like the main and its side brought together as listed?
  9. Guest: Yes, together. I was looking for a bigger salad, but the extra cost changes my choice.
  10. Server: I'll keep the standard main with its included side and no separately charged starter.
Today's special has a price

A server describes a special before the diner commits.

  1. Server: Today's special is a roasted vegetable tart with a green salad, priced at nineteen dollars.
  2. Guest: Is it part of the lunch set, or did you mean a separate special?
  3. Server: It's a separate item today. The lunch set choices are printed in this other section.
  4. Guest: Thank you. Does the special come with a drink, or is that priced separately?
  5. Server: Drinks are separate. The nineteen dollars covers the tart and its accompanying green salad.
  6. Guest: Could you compare that with the twenty-two-dollar lunch set before I choose between them?
  7. Server: The set includes a main and a drink, but its main choices don't include the tart.
  8. Guest: I'd rather have the tart and tap water, so please order the special for me.
  9. Server: Certainly. That's the nineteen-dollar special with tap water, and no set menu on the order.
  10. Guest: Exactly. Thanks for giving me the price before I had to ask about it.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The steak set or steak alone?
  1. Check the answers, then read the menu exchange with a partner. Stress twenty-four plus five and twenty-nine versus twenty-seven.
  2. Switch roles. Repeat the final comparison, keeping potatoes in both options and dessert in the set only.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Answer and explanation

B. twenty-four

Twenty dollars plus the four-dollar supplement equals twenty-four dollars.

2. Guest: "Fish alone is ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

A. twenty-two

The stated a la carte price is twenty-two dollars without dessert.

3. Server: "Both options include ___."

Answer and explanation

C. rice

Rice is included in both options under the supplied menu.

4. Guest: "The separate dessert price is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unspecified

The bundle comparison does not supply a standalone dessert price.

Lesson 02: Explaining menu choices and prices
Original fictional case: The fictional lunch set is $24 and includes a main and dessert. Choosing steak adds a $5 supplement to that set price. The a la carte steak is $27 without dessert. Both steak options include potatoes. Guest Jordan initially thinks the steak set must cost $24, then wonders whether potatoes cost extra. Server Priya must compare the $29 steak set with the $27 steak alone, explain the two-dollar difference, and avoid inventing a separate dessert price, drink inclusion, or tax policy. No choice has been made at the start.
Language workshop: The steak set or steak alone?
Communication goal: Explain a supplement, calculate comparable menu prices, and leave the purchase choice with the guest.
Language guidance: The $5 supplement belongs to the $24 set base: $24 + $5 = $29. It is not added to the $27 a la carte price. The comparison is $29 with dessert versus $27 without dessert, with potatoes in both.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The lunch set includes a main and dessert. / The set starts at $24. / Steak carries a $5 supplement on that set. / That makes the steak set $29. / The a la carte steak is $27. / The a la carte option does not include dessert. / Both steak options include potatoes. / The set is two dollars more than steak alone. / That difference is not a quoted separate dessert price. / The $24 is the base, before the steak supplement. / No extra potato charge is listed in these options. / Would you prefer the set with dessert or the steak alone? / It depends on whether you want the included dessert. / I would need to check any detail not stated on this menu. / Let me confirm which option you would like. / $29 with dessert, or $27 without dessert; potatoes are included in both.
Vocabulary: set menu: Group of included courses offered for a stated price, subject to listed conditions. / a la carte: Ordered as individual menu items rather than as a set. / supplement: Additional charge added to a stated base price. / base price: Starting price before specified additions. / inclusive price: Price covering the items or charges explicitly named as included. / inclusion: Item or service covered by the quoted option. / exclusion: Item or charge not covered by an option. / price difference: Amount separating two prices on a comparable basis. / standalone price: Price charged for an item bought separately. / upgrade: Change to a different or enhanced option, often at an added cost. / main-and-dessert set: Meal option combining a main course and dessert. / side dish: Food served alongside a principal dish. / portion: Amount served to one person or as specified. / menu description: Written account of a dish or offer. / daily special: Dish or offer identified as special for the relevant service period. / availability: Whether an item can currently be ordered. / value comparison: Assessment of price alongside the contents or benefits of an option. / additional charge: Amount payable beyond a specified quoted price. / price clarification: Explanation resolving uncertainty about what an amount means. / itemized total: Sum showing the individual components that make up the charge. / optional extra: Additional item that is not compulsory or automatically included. / menu pairing: Combination of items offered or suggested together. / price basis: Items and conditions to which a quoted price applies. / guest choice: Selection made by the diner after receiving relevant information.

Repeat the final comparison, keeping potatoes in both options and dessert in the set only. Complete and check the fish-set transfer. Read both prices aloud; do not turn the bundle difference into a standalone dessert price.
Partner: Jordan: Lunch guest / Priya: Server

Optional second-round challenge: A fictional main-and-dessert set has a $20 base and a $4 fish supplement. Fish alone costs $22 without dessert. Both options include rice. No separate dessert price is supplied.

Priya: Then the price basis is twenty-nine for steak, potatoes, and the included dessert, or twenty-seven for steak and potatoes without dessert.

Priya: Certainly. I will wait for your guest choice. When you are ready, tell me set or a la carte, and I will confirm the option before entering it.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
