03Receiving an allergy questionChecking the bun before ordering+

Your goal

Receive a reported allergy, ask the kitchen a precise question, and communicate unresolved ingredient and preparation information.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Checking the bun before ordering

At table 6, guest Eli reports a sesame allergy and asks whether the burger bun is suitable. Server Noor has an old menu description, not current ingredient or cross-contact information. Kitchen lead Cam is available to discuss the question before any order is sent. The conversation does not supply a verified bun ingredient list, a confirmed preparation arrangement, or a safe alternative. Noor must preserve the reported allergen, check the current product and handling information through the real allergy procedure, and avoid replacing an unanswered question with reassurance.

Eli: Guest reporting a sesame allergy / Noor: Server

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which allergen does Eli report?

Answer and explanation

D. Sesame

Eli explicitly reports a sesame allergy in the brief.

2. What information does Noor hold?

Answer and explanation

A. An old menu description

The available description is old and does not establish current ingredients or handling.

3. Who is available before an order is sent?

Answer and explanation

D. Kitchen lead Cam

Cam is identified as available to discuss the allergy question.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A plain-looking bun is not an ingredient list. Ingredient information and cross-contact information answer related but different questions. Removing visible seeds does not establish suitability. An old menu description cannot certify the actual product being prepared today.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
food allergy
Immune-system reaction to a food protein that can be serious.report a food allergy
allergen
Substance capable of causing an allergic reaction in a susceptible person.identify the allergen
sesame
Seed recognized among the major food allergens in the United States.flag a sesame allergy
ingredient list
Record of substances intentionally used in a product.check the current ingredient list
product specification
Detailed information describing a particular supplied food product.verify the product specification
supplier label
Label information attached to or associated with a supplied product.inspect the supplier label
recipe
Specified ingredients and method for making a dish.check the current recipe
cross-contact
Unintended introduction of an allergen into another food.assess allergen cross-contact
shared equipment
Equipment used for more than one food or preparation.check shared equipment
preparation area
Place where food is handled or assembled.verify the preparation area
allergen matrix
Structured record showing allergen information across relevant menu items.consult the current allergen matrix
current product
Actual product presently used, which may differ from an older description.identify the current product
recipe change
Alteration to a dish's ingredients or method.verify a recipe change
product substitution
Use of a different supplied item in place of the usual one.check for a product substitution
allergy flag
Clear system marker identifying a reported allergy for the relevant order.enter an allergy flag
kitchen lead
Responsible kitchen colleague coordinating the relevant preparation question.consult the kitchen lead
order hold
Pause before progressing an order while a necessary matter is resolved.maintain the order hold
ingredient verification
Check of the ingredients in the actual relevant product.complete ingredient verification
handling information
Details about how food is stored, prepared, and served.obtain handling information
unsupported assurance
Reassuring claim without adequate information or authority.avoid an unsupported assurance
reported allergen
Substance the guest identifies as the source of an allergy.repeat the reported allergen
alternative dish
Different menu item that still requires appropriate checks for the guest.check an alternative dish
allergic reaction
Response to an allergen that may require urgent medical attention.follow allergic-reaction procedures
emergency response
Immediate action through the applicable emergency arrangements.activate the emergency response

No reaction or exposure occurs in this scenario. Do not invent treatment instructions or tell a guest to test the food. Actual symptoms or urgent concerns require the appropriate emergency response, not completion of a routine menu inquiry.

C Notice the language

Take the disclosure seriously

Thank the guest for identifying the allergy and make the next step clear. Do not test whether the guest sounds serious enough or rely on how a product looks. The guest should hear what is known, what needs checking, and who is handling the question.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the disclosure
Thank you for telling me about the sesame allergy.
Repeat the allergen
I have heard sesame; is that correct?
State the information limit
This old description does not verify today's bun.
Pause the order
I will check before sending an order.
Name the next person
I will speak with Cam, our kitchen lead.
Ask about the actual product
Which bun is being used for this service?
Ask about the record
Can you verify its current ingredient information?
Include handling
We also need the relevant cross-contact information.
Avoid appearance-based reassurance
A plain surface does not establish the ingredients.
Do not improvise a fix
Removing visible seeds would not answer the safety question.
Keep the guest informed
The question is still being checked.
Avoid a false alternative
A different dish would need its own appropriate checks.
Preserve the allergy flag
The reported sesame allergy must stay linked to your order.
Separate receipt and resolution
Cam has received the question; it is not resolved yet.
Avoid unsupported certainty
I cannot confirm suitability from the information available.
Return to the guest accurately
I will explain the verified information and any remaining limits before you choose.
6 language and precision notes
Thank you for telling me
Receives the information without questioning the guest's credibility.
Today's bun
Directs the check to the actual product, not an outdated menu.
Before sending
Places verification ahead of order transmission.
Also
Makes clear that handling is an additional issue, not answered by ingredients alone.
Still being checked
Gives an honest interim status.
Cannot confirm
Marks an information limit without inventing a safe or unsafe verdict.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which first response is best?

Answer and explanation

B. Thank you for telling me; I will check the current bun and handling information with Cam.

The response acknowledges the allergy and directs the unresolved questions to current product and preparation checks.

2. Which distinction matters?

Answer and explanation

B. Ingredients and cross-contact are separate questions.

A product's intended ingredients do not alone answer how unintended allergen contact is managed.

3. What does Cam's availability establish?

Answer and explanation

A. A kitchen lead can discuss the query.

Availability provides a responsible contact, not a completed product or preparation assessment.

4. Which guest update is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. I cannot confirm suitability yet; the current information is being checked.

The update preserves the unresolved status without offering a false guarantee or inventing a clinical event.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Checking the bun before ordering

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Eli: Guest reporting a sesame allergy / Noor: Server

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • reported allergen
  • product specification
  • ingredient verification
  • order hold
  • handling information
  • ingredient list
  • current product
  • allergy flag
  • cross-contact
  • alternative dish
  1. Eli
    Before I order, I need to ask about the burger bun. I have a sesame allergy, and the picture does not tell me what is in the bread.
  2. Noor
    Thank you for telling me. I will repeat the 1 when I speak with the kitchen: sesame. Is that correct, and is the bun the item you want me to check?
  3. Eli
    Yes, sesame, and I am asking about the bun first. I know some bread can contain it even without seeds on the surface.
  4. Noor
    I have only an old description here, not verified information for the 2. I will check with Cam, our kitchen lead, before sending an order.
  5. Eli
    Please include how it is handled, not just the ingredients. I would rather wait for an accurate answer than have someone tell me it is probably fine.
  6. Noor
    Of course. I will ask about the ingredients and 3. I cannot confirm suitability from this description, and I will not put the order through while that question is unresolved.
  7. Eli
    Have you been able to pass that question to Cam? I would like to know it has reached the person checking the kitchen information.
  8. Noor
    Yes, Cam has received it. We need the current 4 for today's bun. That check has not been completed, so the old description is not our answer.
  9. Eli
    Good. Please ask about preparation as well. I have had to be careful with shared equipment at other restaurants.
  10. Noor
    Yes. The relevant 5 still needs verification through our allergy procedure. I made clear that you are asking about both ingredients and preparation.
  11. Eli
    Thank you. I also do not want anyone to remove visible seeds and assume that has answered the question. The bread itself matters.
  12. Noor
    Agreed. We need the actual 6 and relevant supplier information. A plain surface or removal of visible seeds is not enough to establish suitability.
  13. Eli
    I will wait. Please do not bring me a different dish in the meantime; I need to choose after the check.
  14. Noor
    Understood. The 7 remains in place. Any alternative would need its own appropriate checks and your agreement before we proceed.
  15. Eli
    Has the kitchen actually checked the bun yet, or are they still finding the information?
  16. Noor
    Certainly. The 8 and preparation checks are not complete yet. I will keep their actual status clear rather than describe Cam receiving the question as a final answer.
  17. Eli
    If the bun does not work for me, could we discuss another option afterward? I have not picked one yet.
  18. Noor
    I will not. Any 9 would need its own checks and your choice. Your order has not been sent.
  19. Eli
    That is fine. I will wait for the verified information before deciding. Please keep the sesame allergy attached to the discussion if someone else takes over.
  20. Noor
    Yes. The 10 and the unresolved question must stay together. I will return with the information we can actually confirm and explain any remaining limits.

Answers and explanations
  1. reported allergen Turn 2. The reported allergen is sesame, which must be communicated accurately rather than replaced by a vague dietary note.
  2. current product Turn 4. The current product is the bun actually used today; an old description cannot establish its present ingredients.
  3. cross-contact Turn 6. Cross-contact concerns unintended allergen introduction, which is not answered solely by the bun's intended ingredient list.
  4. ingredient list Turn 8. The ingredient list must match the actual bun in use, rather than an older menu or another supplied product.
  5. handling information Turn 10. Handling information covers preparation and service conditions, making it distinct from the list of intentional ingredients.
  6. product specification Turn 12. The product specification describes the supplied item; visual appearance alone is not equivalent to verified product information.
  7. order hold Turn 14. The order hold prevents this unresolved allergy query from being treated as an ordinary confirmed order ready for preparation.
  8. ingredient verification Turn 16. Ingredient verification is the actual check of the relevant product; receiving the question does not mean that check is complete.
  9. alternative dish Turn 18. An alternative dish is a separate option requiring appropriate checks, not an automatically safe replacement for the bun.
  10. allergy flag Turn 20. The allergy flag preserves the reported sesame allergy with the relevant order and handoff; it does not itself certify suitability.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Eli: Before I order, I need to ask about the burger bun. I have a sesame allergy, and the picture does not tell me what is in the bread.
  2. Noor: Thank you for telling me. I will repeat the reported allergen when I speak with the kitchen: sesame. Is that correct, and is the bun the item you want me to check?
  3. Eli: Yes, sesame, and I am asking about the bun first. I know some bread can contain it even without seeds on the surface.
  4. Noor: I have only an old description here, not verified information for the current product. I will check with Cam, our kitchen lead, before sending an order.
  5. Eli: Please include how it is handled, not just the ingredients. I would rather wait for an accurate answer than have someone tell me it is probably fine.
  6. Noor: Of course. I will ask about the ingredients and cross-contact. I cannot confirm suitability from this description, and I will not put the order through while that question is unresolved.
  7. Eli: Have you been able to pass that question to Cam? I would like to know it has reached the person checking the kitchen information.
  8. Noor: Yes, Cam has received it. We need the current ingredient list for today's bun. That check has not been completed, so the old description is not our answer.
  9. Eli: Good. Please ask about preparation as well. I have had to be careful with shared equipment at other restaurants.
  10. Noor: Yes. The relevant handling information still needs verification through our allergy procedure. I made clear that you are asking about both ingredients and preparation.
  11. Eli: Thank you. I also do not want anyone to remove visible seeds and assume that has answered the question. The bread itself matters.
  12. Noor: Agreed. We need the actual product specification and relevant supplier information. A plain surface or removal of visible seeds is not enough to establish suitability.
  13. Eli: I will wait. Please do not bring me a different dish in the meantime; I need to choose after the check.
  14. Noor: Understood. The order hold remains in place. Any alternative would need its own appropriate checks and your agreement before we proceed.
  15. Eli: Has the kitchen actually checked the bun yet, or are they still finding the information?
  16. Noor: Certainly. The ingredient verification and preparation checks are not complete yet. I will keep their actual status clear rather than describe Cam receiving the question as a final answer.
  17. Eli: If the bun does not work for me, could we discuss another option afterward? I have not picked one yet.
  18. Noor: I will not. Any alternative dish would need its own checks and your choice. Your order has not been sent.
  19. Eli: That is fine. I will wait for the verified information before deciding. Please keep the sesame allergy attached to the discussion if someone else takes over.
  20. Noor: Yes. The allergy flag and the unresolved question must stay together. I will return with the information we can actually confirm and explain any remaining limits.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The bun description is incomplete

Table 6: the sesame question from the case.

  1. Guest: I have a sesame allergy. Your bun description doesn't mention seeds; can I eat it?
  2. Server: Thank you for telling me. That description doesn't confirm the current ingredients or handling arrangements.
  3. Guest: I've had buns here before, although I don't remember whether this is the same one.
  4. Server: I can't confirm this bun from your earlier visit. I'll ask the kitchen lead for today's ingredient list.
  5. Guest: Please ask about sesame in the bread itself, not only the seeds on top.
  6. Server: I'll pass on both that ingredient question and your concern about possible cross-contact.
  7. Guest: Should I choose the burger now, or wait until those details have been checked?
  8. Server: Let's leave the food order unsent while we get the information you need to decide.
  9. Guest: All right. Please bring the kitchen lead if the answer needs a longer explanation.
  10. Server: I will ask them to speak with you; your sesame question is still unresolved.
An omission isn't an allergy label

A diner requests no coriander for taste, and the server checks meaning.

  1. Server: You asked for no coriander on the salad. Is that a preference or an allergy?
  2. Guest: It's a dietary preference. I don't enjoy coriander, but I'm not reporting an allergy.
  3. Server: Thank you. I'll record a preference to omit the coriander garnish, subject to the kitchen's confirmation.
  4. Guest: Does that mean you can remove every trace of coriander from the whole dish?
  5. Server: Not necessarily. The dressing may already contain it, and I haven't checked that recipe yet.
  6. Guest: Then could you check the dressing too? I'd choose another salad if it's mixed in.
  7. Server: Certainly. Is the concern the coriander flavor throughout the dish rather than the garnish alone?
  8. Guest: Exactly. I wouldn't want you to change the garnish while leaving the same flavor underneath.
  9. Server: I'll ask that specific question before entering the salad, then explain the available choices.
  10. Guest: Thanks. We can keep my order open while my friend orders their own meal.
Repeating the guest's actual words

A server speaks to the kitchen lead about a pending order.

  1. Server: Table nine reports a milk allergy and asks whether today's soup contains any milk ingredients.
  2. Kitchen lead: Have you sent the soup order already, or are they waiting for our answer?
  3. Server: They're waiting. I said I'd check the actual recipe and handling information before they choose.
  4. Kitchen lead: Good. Was their question only about a cream garnish, or the whole soup preparation?
  5. Server: The whole preparation. They specifically asked whether removing the garnish would settle the issue.
  6. Kitchen lead: I need the current recipe and allergen information; removing a garnish wouldn't settle that.
  7. Server: I'll keep the soup pending. Would you speak directly with them after checking those details?
  8. Kitchen lead: Yes. Tell them I'm gathering the current information and will come to their table.
  9. Server: I'll do that, without describing the soup as suitable before your discussion with the guest.
  10. Kitchen lead: Thank you. Please give me the table number again so I don't approach the wrong party.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Checking the bun before ordering
  1. Check the cloze answers. Read the conversation, keeping sesame clear in every handoff and update.
  2. Switch roles. Stress the difference between Cam receiving the question and completing the ingredient and handling checks.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Receive a different allergen question

At table 9, guest Alex reports a peanut allergy and asks about a sauce. The server has an old description only. Kitchen lead Imani accepts the query. No ingredient or handling answer is yet verified.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Server: "The reported allergen is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. peanut

Alex reports peanut, which must be repeated without changing the allergen.

2. Colleague: "The product being questioned is the ___."

Answer and explanation

D. sauce

The inquiry concerns the sauce, not an invented bread or dessert.

3. Server: "The receiving kitchen lead is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. Imani

Imani explicitly accepts the query as the named kitchen lead.

4. Colleague: "Suitability is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

A. confirmed

Neither current ingredients nor handling has been verified in this exchange.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 03: Receiving an allergy question
Original fictional case: At table 6, guest Eli reports a sesame allergy and asks whether the burger bun is suitable. Server Noor has an old menu description, not current ingredient or cross-contact information. Kitchen lead Cam is available to discuss the question before any order is sent. The conversation does not supply a verified bun ingredient list, a confirmed preparation arrangement, or a safe alternative. Noor must preserve the reported allergen, check the current product and handling information through the real allergy procedure, and avoid replacing an unanswered question with reassurance.
Language workshop: Checking the bun before ordering
Communication goal: Receive a reported allergy, ask the kitchen a precise question, and communicate unresolved ingredient and preparation information.
Language guidance: A plain-looking bun is not an ingredient list. Ingredient information and cross-contact information answer related but different questions. Removing visible seeds does not establish suitability. An old menu description cannot certify the actual product being prepared today.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Thank you for telling me about the sesame allergy. / I have heard sesame; is that correct? / This old description does not verify today's bun. / I will check before sending an order. / I will speak with Cam, our kitchen lead. / Which bun is being used for this service? / Can you verify its current ingredient information? / We also need the relevant cross-contact information. / A plain surface does not establish the ingredients. / Removing visible seeds would not answer the safety question. / The question is still being checked. / A different dish would need its own appropriate checks. / The reported sesame allergy must stay linked to your order. / Cam has received the question; it is not resolved yet. / I cannot confirm suitability from the information available. / I will explain the verified information and any remaining limits before you choose.
Vocabulary: food allergy: Immune-system reaction to a food protein that can be serious. / allergen: Substance capable of causing an allergic reaction in a susceptible person. / sesame: Seed recognized among the major food allergens in the United States. / ingredient list: Record of substances intentionally used in a product. / product specification: Detailed information describing a particular supplied food product. / supplier label: Label information attached to or associated with a supplied product. / recipe: Specified ingredients and method for making a dish. / cross-contact: Unintended introduction of an allergen into another food. / shared equipment: Equipment used for more than one food or preparation. / preparation area: Place where food is handled or assembled. / allergen matrix: Structured record showing allergen information across relevant menu items. / current product: Actual product presently used, which may differ from an older description. / recipe change: Alteration to a dish's ingredients or method. / product substitution: Use of a different supplied item in place of the usual one. / allergy flag: Clear system marker identifying a reported allergy for the relevant order. / kitchen lead: Responsible kitchen colleague coordinating the relevant preparation question. / order hold: Pause before progressing an order while a necessary matter is resolved. / ingredient verification: Check of the ingredients in the actual relevant product. / handling information: Details about how food is stored, prepared, and served. / unsupported assurance: Reassuring claim without adequate information or authority. / reported allergen: Substance the guest identifies as the source of an allergy. / alternative dish: Different menu item that still requires appropriate checks for the guest. / allergic reaction: Response to an allergen that may require urgent medical attention. / emergency response: Immediate action through the applicable emergency arrangements.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Coordinating courses and delaysEight more minutes?+

Your goal

Explain a kitchen estimate against a guest departure time without converting readiness into a guaranteed meal completion.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Eight more minutes?

Table 14 has finished its starters. The mains were fired at 7:20. At 7:30, the kitchen estimates another eight minutes because one dish is still being prepared. The guests need to leave at 7:50. Server Tomas must explain the current estimate and offer a discussion about timing, while avoiding a promise that the mains will arrive exactly at 7:38 or that the guests can finish comfortably before departure. No cancellation, faster substitute, priority slot, or price adjustment has been approved in this case.

Tomas: Server / Riley: Guest at table 14

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. When were the mains fired?

Answer and explanation

D. 7:20

The kitchen began the relevant preparation stage at the stated 7:20 time.

2. What is the current estimate at 7:30?

Answer and explanation

B. Another eight minutes

The estimate is eight additional minutes from the 7:30 update.

3. What is the guests' departure requirement?

Answer and explanation

C. Leave at 7:50

The guests explicitly need to leave at 7:50.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Eight more minutes at 7:30 points to approximately 7:38 for the stated readiness estimate. It is not eight minutes from 7:20. Readiness, delivery to the table, eating, payment, and departure are separate events.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
fire
Kitchen instruction to begin or advance preparation at the required service stage.fire the mains
fired time
Recorded time when the relevant preparation instruction was given.confirm the fired time
pickup
Kitchen-to-service handoff when dishes are ready for collection.coordinate pickup
pass
Kitchen area where finished dishes are checked and handed to service staff.collect from the pass
expeditor
Person coordinating dish completion and kitchen-to-floor delivery.check with the expeditor
course pacing
Timing of successive stages of a meal.adjust course pacing
ticket time
Elapsed time associated with an order ticket, measured from a specified event.check the ticket time
readiness estimate
Approximate expectation for when food will be ready at the stated stage.give a readiness estimate
remaining time
Additional duration expected from the current update.state the remaining time
departure deadline
Latest time by which guests need to leave.acknowledge the departure deadline
service window
Period available for serving or completing a specified service activity.discuss the service window
preparation stage
Current step in making a dish.confirm the preparation stage
table update
Information given to guests about their order or service.deliver a table update
timing constraint
Limit that affects when something needs to occur.clarify the timing constraint
bottleneck
Step limiting the progress of the overall process.identify the bottleneck
coordinated service
Arrangement for related dishes to reach guests in a planned sequence.arrange coordinated service
rush request
Request to accelerate work that still requires confirmation.check a rush request
priority
Relative precedence given to a task under the actual service arrangement.confirm priority
on the fly
Urgent preparation request in restaurant shorthand, not a guarantee of instant service.request a dish on the fly
elapsed time
Duration already passed between specified points.calculate elapsed time
update point
Agreed occasion for communicating the next status.agree an update point
timing discussion
Conversation about feasible options under a time constraint.offer a timing discussion
completion promise
Commitment that a stated outcome will occur by a defined time.avoid an unsupported completion promise
contingency
Alternative arrangement available if the intended plan cannot work.verify a contingency

The twelve minutes between 7:38 and 7:50 is a clock difference, not proof of a comfortable meal. Confirm what the kitchen estimate covers and discuss actual options. Do not promise an accelerated dish or cancellation before the appropriate decision is made.

C Notice the language

Tell the guest what the clock actually means

Internal timing can sound like a promise when repeated without context. Translate preparation status into guest-facing language, acknowledge the departure constraint, and check realistic options. Do not hide uncertainty behind kitchen jargon or assume the guest has enough time to eat.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the wait
I am sorry your mains are taking longer than you expected.
Give the current update
At 7:30, the kitchen estimates another eight minutes.
Name the remaining work
One dish is still being prepared.
Translate the estimate
That points to around 7:38, not a guaranteed service time.
Preserve the source
This is the kitchen's current estimate.
Repeat the constraint
You need to leave at 7:50.
Avoid a false comfort claim
I cannot promise that leaves enough time for your meal.
Separate stages
Kitchen readiness and arrival at the table are different stages.
Offer a discussion
Let me discuss the timing options with you and the kitchen.
Avoid invented priority
I cannot promise a faster slot before it is confirmed.
Clarify the clock
The eight minutes is from the 7:30 update.
Keep the earlier time clear
The mains were fired at 7:20.
Do not promise a substitute
Any alternative timing needs to be checked.
Agree the next communication
Let us agree when you will receive the next update.
Do not dismiss the deadline
Your departure time matters to the decision.
Close accurately
The estimate is still provisional, and the timing options need discussion.
6 language and precision notes
Another
Marks additional time from the current update.
Around
Signals approximation rather than a fixed appointment.
Current estimate
Allows for a changed status without pretending certainty.
Need to leave
Identifies a departure constraint, not a food-readiness target.
Before it is confirmed
Keeps a request distinct from an accepted commitment.
Enough time
Requires the guest's practical needs, not arithmetic alone.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which timing explanation is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. At 7:30 the kitchen estimates eight more minutes, pointing to around 7:38.

The explanation anchors the additional eight minutes to the current update and retains uncertainty.

2. What does 7:50 minus 7:38 establish?

Answer and explanation

D. A twelve-minute clock interval, not guaranteed dining time

Arithmetic alone does not establish delivery, eating, payment, or a comfortable departure.

3. Which offer stays within the facts?

Answer and explanation

A. I can discuss the timing options with you and the kitchen.

A timing discussion is available without inventing an approved operational or financial change.

4. Which phrase needs translation for a guest?

Answer and explanation

B. Your mains were fired at 7:20.

Fired is internal kitchen shorthand and may need a plain-language explanation of the preparation stage.

E Conversations

Eight more minutes?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Tomas: Server / Riley: Guest at table 14

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • rush request
  • timing discussion
  • table update
  • timing constraint
  • Pickup
  • readiness estimate
  • departure deadline
  • completion promise
  • remaining time
  • fired time
  1. Riley
    Could you check our mains, please? We have finished the starters, and we need to leave at seven fifty. I am beginning to worry about the time.
  2. Tomas
    I am sorry about the wait. I have a current 1 from the kitchen: at seven thirty, they expect another eight minutes. One dish is still being prepared.
  3. Riley
    Another eight minutes from now? I thought the mains had gone in earlier. I am trying to work out whether we can stay.
  4. Tomas
    The 2 is from the seven-thirty update. That points to around seven thirty-eight for the stated estimate, but I cannot guarantee arrival at that exact time.
  5. Riley
    When did they start preparing them? We are not upset with you, but we do need to know where we stand.
  6. Tomas
    The 3 was seven twenty. In our kitchen language, that is when the mains were called for preparation. It does not mean they were ready or served then.
  7. Riley
    All right. If they are ready around seven thirty-eight, that leaves twelve minutes before we have to go. That sounds rather tight for us.
  8. Tomas
    I agree that your 4 matters. The twelve minutes is only the difference between those times; it does not guarantee enough time to receive the food, eat, and settle the bill.
  9. Riley
    Can you move our order ahead of everyone else? I realize the kitchen is busy, but we cannot miss the event we are going to.
  10. Tomas
    I can discuss a 5 with the kitchen. I cannot promise a different position or faster completion before they confirm what is actually possible.
  11. Riley
    Would it be quicker to change one of the dishes? I do not want to cancel the whole meal if a realistic option is available.
  12. Tomas
    We can have a 6 about that. I would need the kitchen to check any alternative; I do not have a confirmed substitute time to offer you now.
  13. Riley
    Please check. We would rather hear what is possible now than sit here hoping the food will arrive.
  14. Tomas
    Understood. My next 7 should tell you what the kitchen can confirm and what remains uncertain. We should also agree when you will hear from me again.
  15. Riley
    Does that time mean ready in the kitchen or actually at our table? We will still need time to eat and pay.
  16. Tomas
    Correct. 8 and delivery to the table are separate from the cooking estimate. I will clarify which stage the estimate covers when I speak with the kitchen.
  17. Riley
    Thank you. We still need to leave at seven fifty, even if the food is delayed. That is the constraint we need you to work with.
  18. Tomas
    I will carry that 9 into the conversation. I will not represent your departure as flexible or assume you can stay longer.
  19. Riley
    Thank you. Please check before changing anything. We still have the original order for now.
  20. Tomas
    Agreed. I will avoid a 10 I cannot support. Your order status, the current estimate, and any decision we make next will stay clear.

Answers and explanations
  1. readiness estimate Turn 2. The readiness estimate is provisional information about food readiness, not a promise that the whole meal will finish on time.
  2. remaining time Turn 4. Remaining time means additional time from the seven-thirty update, not total time since preparation began.
  3. fired time Turn 6. The fired time records the preparation instruction at seven twenty; it is distinct from the later readiness estimate.
  4. departure deadline Turn 8. The departure deadline is seven fifty, which must be considered separately from preparation or service estimates.
  5. rush request Turn 10. A rush request asks for acceleration; it does not establish accepted priority or a faster confirmed completion time.
  6. timing discussion Turn 12. A timing discussion explores feasible options without claiming that a substitute or cancellation has already been approved.
  7. table update Turn 14. A table update should communicate the actual order status and options, not repeat reassurance unsupported by the kitchen.
  8. Pickup Turn 16. Pickup is the kitchen-to-service handoff, which remains distinct from delivery to the table and completion of the meal.
  9. timing constraint Turn 18. The timing constraint remains the guests' seven-fifty departure; an uncertain kitchen estimate does not remove it.
  10. completion promise Turn 20. A completion promise would commit to a finished outcome; the available estimate does not justify such a guarantee.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Riley: Could you check our mains, please? We have finished the starters, and we need to leave at seven fifty. I am beginning to worry about the time.
  2. Tomas: I am sorry about the wait. I have a current readiness estimate from the kitchen: at seven thirty, they expect another eight minutes. One dish is still being prepared.
  3. Riley: Another eight minutes from now? I thought the mains had gone in earlier. I am trying to work out whether we can stay.
  4. Tomas: The remaining time is from the seven-thirty update. That points to around seven thirty-eight for the stated estimate, but I cannot guarantee arrival at that exact time.
  5. Riley: When did they start preparing them? We are not upset with you, but we do need to know where we stand.
  6. Tomas: The fired time was seven twenty. In our kitchen language, that is when the mains were called for preparation. It does not mean they were ready or served then.
  7. Riley: All right. If they are ready around seven thirty-eight, that leaves twelve minutes before we have to go. That sounds rather tight for us.
  8. Tomas: I agree that your departure deadline matters. The twelve minutes is only the difference between those times; it does not guarantee enough time to receive the food, eat, and settle the bill.
  9. Riley: Can you move our order ahead of everyone else? I realize the kitchen is busy, but we cannot miss the event we are going to.
  10. Tomas: I can discuss a rush request with the kitchen. I cannot promise a different position or faster completion before they confirm what is actually possible.
  11. Riley: Would it be quicker to change one of the dishes? I do not want to cancel the whole meal if a realistic option is available.
  12. Tomas: We can have a timing discussion about that. I would need the kitchen to check any alternative; I do not have a confirmed substitute time to offer you now.
  13. Riley: Please check. We would rather hear what is possible now than sit here hoping the food will arrive.
  14. Tomas: Understood. My next table update should tell you what the kitchen can confirm and what remains uncertain. We should also agree when you will hear from me again.
  15. Riley: Does that time mean ready in the kitchen or actually at our table? We will still need time to eat and pay.
  16. Tomas: Correct. Pickup and delivery to the table are separate from the cooking estimate. I will clarify which stage the estimate covers when I speak with the kitchen.
  17. Riley: Thank you. We still need to leave at seven fifty, even if the food is delayed. That is the constraint we need you to work with.
  18. Tomas: I will carry that timing constraint into the conversation. I will not represent your departure as flexible or assume you can stay longer.
  19. Riley: Thank you. Please check before changing anything. We still have the original order for now.
  20. Tomas: Agreed. I will avoid a completion promise I cannot support. Your order status, the current estimate, and any decision we make next will stay clear.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Eight more minutes

Table 14 at 7:30, following the timing case.

  1. Server: I've checked your mains. One dish is still being prepared; the estimate is another eight minutes.
  2. Guest: We ordered a while ago. Does that mean eight minutes from when you first sent it?
  3. Server: No, eight more minutes from this update. Your mains were fired at seven twenty.
  4. Guest: We need to leave at seven fifty. Is the eight-minute serving time definite now?
  5. Server: It's the kitchen's current estimate, not a guarantee. I'll make your departure time clear to them.
  6. Guest: Please do. We need time to eat, not simply have the plates arrive before we leave.
  7. Server: Understood. Would you prefer to wait on that estimate, or ask about another available option?
  8. Guest: Let's wait for now, but please tell us promptly if the kitchen's estimate changes again.
  9. Server: I will. I'll bring the bill when requested, so payment doesn't add avoidable delay.
  10. Guest: Please bring it with the mains. Let us know if the wait gets longer.
Holding dessert for a call

A diner requests a pause between the main and dessert.

  1. Guest: Could you hold our desserts? My colleague has stepped outside to take a work call.
  2. Server: Of course. Do you mean delay both desserts, or cancel one of them altogether?
  3. Guest: Delay both, please. We still want them, but we'd like to eat them together.
  4. Server: I'll ask the dessert station to hold the order if it hasn't already been prepared.
  5. Guest: Can you check that now? I don't want something sitting here while they're outside.
  6. Server: The station confirms the desserts haven't been prepared, so both are now on hold.
  7. Guest: Thank you. Should I wave when we're ready, or place the dessert order again?
  8. Server: Just let me know you're ready. The original order remains; there's no need to duplicate it.
  9. Guest: They're coming back now, but we'd like five more minutes before you request the desserts.
  10. Server: I'll leave the hold in place until you tell me to send that request.
One plate isn't the whole table

A runner checks an incomplete pickup at the pass.

  1. Runner: I see three plates for table eight, but their ticket shows four main courses.
  2. Expediter: The fourth dish is finishing. Please don't describe the entire table as ready yet.
  3. Runner: Should I take these three now, or wait for the complete pickup from you?
  4. Expediter: Wait for my call on this table. I need to coordinate the final dish with the cook.
  5. Runner: The server is asking what to tell the guests. Do you have a current estimate?
  6. Expediter: About three more minutes, but I'll update you if that changes during the pickup.
  7. Runner: I'll tell the server the table isn't complete and the current estimate is three minutes.
  8. Expediter: Thanks. Please keep table eight separate from the two plates beside it for table eighteen.
  9. Runner: These three stay here while we wait for the fourth; table eighteen's two plates stay separate.
  10. Expediter: That's right. I'll call the full table number when each pickup is ready to leave.

At 8:10, the kitchen estimates six more minutes for a dish. The guests need to leave at 8:30. No faster alternative or guaranteed table-delivery time has been confirmed.

Answer and explanation

The supplied deadline is departure at 8:30, not food readiness.

Answer and explanation

No quicker option has been confirmed in this scenario.

Lesson 04: Coordinating courses and delays
Original fictional case: Table 14 has finished its starters. The mains were fired at 7:20. At 7:30, the kitchen estimates another eight minutes because one dish is still being prepared. The guests need to leave at 7:50. Server Tomas must explain the current estimate and offer a discussion about timing, while avoiding a promise that the mains will arrive exactly at 7:38 or that the guests can finish comfortably before departure. No cancellation, faster substitute, priority slot, or price adjustment has been approved in this case.
Language workshop: Eight more minutes?
Communication goal: Explain a kitchen estimate against a guest departure time without converting readiness into a guaranteed meal completion.
Language guidance: Eight more minutes at 7:30 points to approximately 7:38 for the stated readiness estimate. It is not eight minutes from 7:20. Readiness, delivery to the table, eating, payment, and departure are separate events.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I am sorry your mains are taking longer than you expected. / At 7:30, the kitchen estimates another eight minutes. / One dish is still being prepared. / That points to around 7:38, not a guaranteed service time. / This is the kitchen's current estimate. / You need to leave at 7:50. / I cannot promise that leaves enough time for your meal. / Kitchen readiness and arrival at the table are different stages. / Let me discuss the timing options with you and the kitchen. / I cannot promise a faster slot before it is confirmed. / The eight minutes is from the 7:30 update. / The mains were fired at 7:20. / Any alternative timing needs to be checked. / Let us agree when you will receive the next update. / Your departure time matters to the decision. / The estimate is still provisional, and the timing options need discussion.
Vocabulary: fire: Kitchen instruction to begin or advance preparation at the required service stage. / fired time: Recorded time when the relevant preparation instruction was given. / pickup: Kitchen-to-service handoff when dishes are ready for collection. / pass: Kitchen area where finished dishes are checked and handed to service staff. / expeditor: Person coordinating dish completion and kitchen-to-floor delivery. / course pacing: Timing of successive stages of a meal. / ticket time: Elapsed time associated with an order ticket, measured from a specified event. / readiness estimate: Approximate expectation for when food will be ready at the stated stage. / remaining time: Additional duration expected from the current update. / departure deadline: Latest time by which guests need to leave. / service window: Period available for serving or completing a specified service activity. / preparation stage: Current step in making a dish. / table update: Information given to guests about their order or service. / timing constraint: Limit that affects when something needs to occur. / bottleneck: Step limiting the progress of the overall process. / coordinated service: Arrangement for related dishes to reach guests in a planned sequence. / rush request: Request to accelerate work that still requires confirmation. / priority: Relative precedence given to a task under the actual service arrangement. / on the fly: Urgent preparation request in restaurant shorthand, not a guarantee of instant service. / elapsed time: Duration already passed between specified points. / update point: Agreed occasion for communicating the next status. / timing discussion: Conversation about feasible options under a time constraint. / completion promise: Commitment that a stated outcome will occur by a defined time. / contingency: Alternative arrangement available if the intended plan cannot work.
Speaking task: Check the answers and read the delay conversation. Stress seven thirty, another eight minutes, and seven fifty. Switch roles. Repeat the readiness explanation without promising table arrival or enough time to eat. Complete the new-estimate transfer. Check the arithmetic and key, then read both the approximate readiness and fixed departure times.
Partner: Tomas: Server / Riley: Guest at table 14
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: At 8:10, the kitchen estimates six more minutes for a dish. The guests need to leave at 8:30. No faster alternative or guaranteed table-delivery time has been confirmed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Riley: Thank you. We still need to leave at seven fifty, even if the food is delayed. That is the constraint we need you to work with.
Tomas: I will carry that timing constraint into the conversation. I will not represent your departure as flexible or assume you can stay longer.
Riley: Thank you. Please check before changing anything. We still have the original order for now.
Tomas: Agreed. I will avoid a completion promise I cannot support. Your order status, the current estimate, and any decision we make next will stay clear.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

REFERENCE
Course: Restaurant Serving English
Professional audience: Restaurant servers, food runners, and dining-room service staff
Scope: All restaurants, prices, guests, and service events are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not food-safety certification, medical advice, or payment compliance. Lesson 04: Coordinating courses and delays
Original fictional case: Table 14 has finished its starters. The mains were fired at 7:20. At 7:30, the kitchen estimates another eight minutes because one dish is still being prepared. The guests need to leave at 7:50. Server Tomas must explain the current estimate and offer a discussion about timing, while avoiding a promise that the mains will arrive exactly at 7:38 or that the guests can finish comfortably before departure. No cancellation, faster substitute, priority slot, or price adjustment has been approved in this case.
Language workshop: Eight more minutes?
Communication goal: Explain a kitchen estimate against a guest departure time without converting readiness into a guaranteed meal completion.
Language guidance: Eight more minutes at 7:30 points to approximately 7:38 for the stated readiness estimate. It is not eight minutes from 7:20. Readiness, delivery to the table, eating, payment, and departure are separate events.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I am sorry your mains are taking longer than you expected. / At 7:30, the kitchen estimates another eight minutes. / One dish is still being prepared. / That points to around 7:38, not a guaranteed service time. / This is the kitchen's current estimate. / You need to leave at 7:50. / I cannot promise that leaves enough time for your meal. / Kitchen readiness and arrival at the table are different stages. / Let me discuss the timing options with you and the kitchen. / I cannot promise a faster slot before it is confirmed. / The eight minutes is from the 7:30 update. / The mains were fired at 7:20. / Any alternative timing needs to be checked. / Let us agree when you will receive the next update. / Your departure time matters to the decision. / The estimate is still provisional, and the timing options need discussion.
Vocabulary: fire: Kitchen instruction to begin or advance preparation at the required service stage. / fired time: Recorded time when the relevant preparation instruction was given. / pickup: Kitchen-to-service handoff when dishes are ready for collection. / pass: Kitchen area where finished dishes are checked and handed to service staff. / expeditor: Person coordinating dish completion and kitchen-to-floor delivery. / course pacing: Timing of successive stages of a meal. / ticket time: Elapsed time associated with an order ticket, measured from a specified event. / readiness estimate: Approximate expectation for when food will be ready at the stated stage. / remaining time: Additional duration expected from the current update. / departure deadline: Latest time by which guests need to leave. / service window: Period available for serving or completing a specified service activity. / preparation stage: Current step in making a dish. / table update: Information given to guests about their order or service. / timing constraint: Limit that affects when something needs to occur. / bottleneck: Step limiting the progress of the overall process. / coordinated service: Arrangement for related dishes to reach guests in a planned sequence. / rush request: Request to accelerate work that still requires confirmation. / priority: Relative precedence given to a task under the actual service arrangement. / on the fly: Urgent preparation request in restaurant shorthand, not a guarantee of instant service. / elapsed time: Duration already passed between specified points. / update point: Agreed occasion for communicating the next status. / timing discussion: Conversation about feasible options under a time constraint. / completion promise: Commitment that a stated outcome will occur by a defined time. / contingency: Alternative arrangement available if the intended plan cannot work.
Speaking task: Check the answers and read the delay conversation. Stress seven thirty, another eight minutes, and seven fifty. Switch roles. Repeat the readiness explanation without promising table arrival or enough time to eat. Complete the new-estimate transfer. Check the arithmetic and key, then read both the approximate readiness and fixed departure times.
Partner: Tomas: Server / Riley: Guest at table 14
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: At 8:10, the kitchen estimates six more minutes for a dish. The guests need to leave at 8:30. No faster alternative or guaranteed table-delivery time has been confirmed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Riley: Thank you. We still need to leave at seven fifty, even if the food is delayed. That is the constraint we need you to work with.
Tomas: I will carry that timing constraint into the conversation. I will not represent your departure as flexible or assume you can stay longer.
Riley: Thank you. Please check before changing anything. We still have the original order for now.
Tomas: Agreed. I will avoid a completion promise I cannot support. Your order status, the current estimate, and any decision we make next will stay clear.
END REFERENCE
05Correcting a dish without blamePasta ordered, risotto delivered+

Your goal

Acknowledge an order mismatch, offer a specific remedy, and separate replacement timing from a bill-adjustment decision.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

Pasta ordered, risotto delivered

At table 3, guest Alex ordered vegetable pasta but received mushroom risotto. The ticket confirms vegetable pasta. Server Dana checks the mismatch and learns that the kitchen can remake the pasta with an estimated ten-minute wait. Dana may offer that replacement, but any bill adjustment requires shift lead review. The cause of the wrong delivery is not established. No allergy is reported. Alex has not accepted the remake at the start, and neither compensation nor a guaranteed ten-minute arrival has been approved.

Alex: Guest at table 3 / Dana: Server

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the ticket confirm?

Answer and explanation

B. Vegetable pasta

The ticket supports the guest's vegetable-pasta order rather than the dish delivered.

2. What replacement can Dana offer?

Answer and explanation

B. A pasta remake with an estimated ten-minute wait

The kitchen offers the pasta remake, with its timing stated as an estimate.

3. Who must review a bill adjustment?

Answer and explanation

B. The shift lead

The case assigns bill-adjustment review to the shift lead.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The ticket confirms pasta, so the server can acknowledge the mismatch without guessing its cause. The kitchen offers a remake; the guest still needs to accept it. An available remedy is not the same as a completed resolution.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
order mismatch
Difference between what was ordered and what was delivered or recorded.acknowledge the order mismatch
remake
Newly prepared replacement for a dish that needs correction.request a remake
replacement offer
Proposed substitute or corrected item that the guest may accept.explain the replacement offer
service recovery
Actions addressing a service failure and its effect on the guest.manage service recovery
apology
Direct expression of regret for an error or inconvenience.offer a clear apology
inconvenience
Practical difficulty or disruption caused to the guest.acknowledge the inconvenience
ticket check
Review of the order record to establish what was entered.perform a ticket check
delivered dish
Food actually brought to the guest.identify the delivered dish
ordered dish
Food the guest requested.confirm the ordered dish
confirmed mismatch
Discrepancy established by the available record and observation.state the confirmed mismatch
cause
Reason an error occurred, which may require further investigation.avoid guessing the cause
blame
Attribution of fault to a person or group.avoid unsupported blame
remedy
Action intended to put a problem right.offer an available remedy
estimated wait
Approximate expected delay before a specified next event.explain the estimated wait
acceptance
Agreement to a proposed option.confirm acceptance
bill adjustment
Authorized change to an amount on the guest's bill.request a bill adjustment
comp
Informal restaurant term for an item provided without charge under authorization.obtain approval for a comp
discount
Reduction from a stated price.authorize a discount
refund
Return of money already paid.process an approved refund
void
Cancellation of a transaction or entry through the relevant system procedure.record an authorized void
shift lead
Person coordinating service decisions during a shift.consult the shift lead
approval limit
Boundary on the decisions someone may authorize.observe an approval limit
resolution status
Whether the proposed correction has actually been agreed and completed.confirm the resolution status
follow-up check
Later confirmation that the corrective action occurred and met the stated need.make a follow-up check

A remake, comp, discount, void, and refund are different actions. A refund returns paid money; a bill adjustment may happen before payment. Follow actual approval and payment procedures, and do not describe shift-lead review as a completed financial decision.

C Notice the language

An apology can be direct

Name the mismatch without making the guest prove an error already confirmed by the ticket. Take responsibility for the next step without guessing which colleague caused the problem. A sincere apology does not require inventing a free meal or a guaranteed preparation time.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the mismatch
You ordered vegetable pasta, and we brought mushroom risotto.
Apologize directly
I am sorry; that is not the dish you ordered.
Refer to the record
I checked the ticket, and it confirms the pasta.
Avoid blaming the guest
You do not need to explain the order again.
Offer the actual remedy
The kitchen can remake the vegetable pasta.
State the estimated wait
The current estimate is ten minutes.
Ask for acceptance
Would you like that replacement?
Keep timing honest
I cannot guarantee an exact arrival time.
Avoid guessing fault
I have not established how the mismatch happened.
Address the practical effect
I understand that the extra wait affects your meal.
Separate the financial question
Any bill adjustment needs the shift lead's review.
Do not promise an unauthorized comp
I cannot confirm a free item before approval.
Name the next action
I will bring the bill question to the shift lead.
Keep consent clear
I will wait for your go-ahead before arranging the replacement.
Check completion later
I will confirm that the correct dish reaches you.
Confirm the next step
I will check the bill question first, then come back for your decision.
6 language and precision notes
We brought
Acknowledges the service error without assigning unsupported individual blame.
Confirms
Identifies evidence for what was ordered.
Can remake
Describes an available action, not one already completed.
Would you like
Requests the guest's decision.
Current estimate
Preserves uncertainty in preparation timing.
Needs review
Marks a pending decision rather than approval.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which apology fits the facts?

Answer and explanation

A. I am sorry; the ticket confirms pasta, but we brought risotto.

The apology acknowledges the confirmed mismatch without blaming the guest or inventing a cause.

2. Which offer is authorized?

Answer and explanation

D. A pasta remake with an estimated ten-minute wait

Dana may offer the remake, while timing remains estimated and billing requires review.

3. Which statement keeps the financial decision accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. I will ask the shift lead to review the bill question.

The supplied authority covers referral for review, not a completed adjustment or universal rule.

4. When can the issue be described as fully resolved?

Answer and explanation

C. After the relevant agreed actions are actually completed and checked

An apology and offer are steps toward resolution, not evidence that the correction and related decisions are complete.

Pasta ordered, risotto delivered

  2. Dana

    Can I still get the pasta? I had been looking forward to it, but I am worried the others will finish before mine arrives.

    The kitchen can make a 2. Their current estimate is ten minutes. Would you like me to arrange that?
  5. Alex
    Is that ten minutes from now? We have already waited for the first round, so I need a realistic idea before I decide.
  6. Dana
    They have quoted an 3 of ten minutes. I cannot promise the exact arrival time, and I understand another wait disrupts your meal.
  7. Alex
    Will there be anything off the bill? I do not feel comfortable paying as though everything arrived correctly.
  8. Dana
    I can ask for a 4. Our shift lead needs to review that; I can offer the replacement but cannot approve the charge change myself.
  9. Alex
    I thought the replacement might be free. Are you saying you cannot promise that yet?
  10. Dana
    That is right. I cannot promise it will be 5 before the lead decides. I will explain both the wrong dish and the additional wait.
  11. Alex

  12. Dana
    The ticket is correct. I do not yet know the 6 of the delivery error. I can acknowledge it without guessing which colleague made it.
  13. Alex

    Understood. I will wait for your 8. Hearing the offer is not the same as agreeing to it.

  18. Dana
    I will pass that request to the 9, along with the ticket, what arrived, and the ten-minute estimate.
  19. Alex

  20. Dana
    That is our 10. I am sorry your meal has been interrupted. I will return with the decision rather than leave you wondering what is happening.

Answers and explanations
  1. mistake Turn 2. The ticket confirms pasta but risotto arrived, so mistake acknowledges an established service error without assigning blame.
  2. remake Turn 4. A remake is a newly prepared corrected dish, here the vegetable pasta originally ordered.
  3. estimated wait Turn 6. An estimated wait is approximate; Dana cannot turn the kitchen's estimate into a guaranteed arrival time.
  4. bill adjustment Turn 8. A bill adjustment changes the charge and requires the shift lead's review under this case's stated authority.
  5. complimentary Turn 10. Complimentary means provided without charge; the offered remake has not been approved as free.
  6. cause Turn 12. Cause concerns how the delivery error happened; the correct ticket alone does not identify the responsible stage or person.
  7. check back Turn 14. Check back means return to verify the correction and update the guest if the timing changes.
  8. go-ahead Turn 16. The go-ahead is the guest's permission to proceed; Alex has expressly asked Dana not to start the remake yet.
  9. shift lead Turn 18. The shift lead reviews the charge question and can receive Alex's request for a direct conversation.
  10. next step Turn 20. The next step is the bill review and update; neither the financial decision nor Alex's remake choice is complete.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Alex: Excuse me, this is mushroom risotto. I ordered vegetable pasta. Could you check? Everyone else has their main, and I do not want to start the wrong dish.
  2. Dana: I am sorry about the mistake. The ticket says vegetable pasta. We brought you something different.
  3. Alex: Can I still get the pasta? I had been looking forward to it, but I am worried the others will finish before mine arrives.
  4. Dana: The kitchen can make a remake. Their current estimate is ten minutes. Would you like me to arrange that?
  5. Alex: Is that ten minutes from now? We have already waited for the first round, so I need a realistic idea before I decide.
  6. Dana: They have quoted an estimated wait of ten minutes. I cannot promise the exact arrival time, and I understand another wait disrupts your meal.
  7. Alex: Will there be anything off the bill? I do not feel comfortable paying as though everything arrived correctly.
  8. Dana: I can ask for a bill adjustment. Our shift lead needs to review that; I can offer the replacement but cannot approve the charge change myself.
  9. Alex: I thought the replacement might be free. Are you saying you cannot promise that yet?
  10. Dana: That is right. I cannot promise it will be complimentary before the lead decides. I will explain both the wrong dish and the additional wait.
  11. Alex: Was the order entered incorrectly? I heard you say the ticket has pasta on it.
  12. Dana: The ticket is correct. I do not yet know the cause of the delivery error. I can acknowledge it without guessing which colleague made it.
  13. Alex: If I do take the pasta, will you make sure it is mine this time? I would rather not go through this again.
  14. Dana: Yes. I will check back on the corrected dish and keep you updated if the kitchen's timing changes.
  15. Alex: Before I decide, could you speak to the lead about the bill? Please do not start the replacement yet.
  16. Dana: Understood. I will wait for your go-ahead. Hearing the offer is not the same as agreeing to it.
  17. Alex: Thank you. Can the lead speak with me if there are questions? I would prefer to explain what the extra wait means for us.
  18. Dana: I will pass that request to the shift lead, along with the ticket, what arrived, and the ten-minute estimate.
  19. Alex: All right. For now, please check the bill question. Then I will decide whether to wait for the pasta.
  20. Dana: That is our next step. I am sorry your meal has been interrupted. I will return with the decision rather than leave you wondering what is happening.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Risotto instead of pasta

Table 3 receives the wrong main, as in the case.

  1. Guest: This looks like mushroom risotto. I ordered vegetable pasta when you took our main courses.
  2. Server: You're right; the ticket shows vegetable pasta. I'm sorry we brought the wrong dish.
  3. Guest: Can you replace it, and how long would I be waiting for the correct meal?
  4. Server: The kitchen offers a pasta remake with an estimated ten-minute wait. Would that work for you?
  5. Guest: My friend is already eating. Could you remove my dish from the bill as well?
  6. Server: I can arrange the replacement; a bill adjustment needs our shift lead's review.
  7. Guest: Please ask them, but go ahead with the pasta. I'd still like to eat.
  8. Server: I'll send the remake request and ask the lead to speak with you about the charge.
  9. Guest: Thank you. Please tell them I've accepted the replacement, not agreed about the final bill.
  10. Server: I will make that distinction clear, and check back when your pasta reaches the table.
A check-back gets a real answer

A guest says a meal is not what they expected.

  1. Server: How is the tart? Is there anything about your order that needs our attention?
  2. Guest: It's much smaller than I expected. I thought it would fill the whole plate.
  3. Server: I understand. The menu doesn't give a size, so I can see why expectations differed.
  4. Guest: I'm not saying it's badly made. I just need something more substantial for lunch.
  5. Server: Would you like to see the available side dishes and prices before deciding what to add?
  6. Guest: Yes, but please don't order anything automatically. I want to know the total first.
  7. Server: The potato side is four dollars, and the green salad is five dollars today.
  8. Guest: The potatoes sound better. Please add one portion, provided that price is the full item price.
  9. Server: Four dollars is the listed side price. I'll add one portion and confirm its expected wait.
  10. Guest: Thanks. That addresses my concern; I wanted a clearer choice, not a different tart.
Two plates crossed at pickup

A runner discovers that two complete orders were assigned to wrong tables.

  1. Runner: I checked the pickup tickets and think I brought table seven's tart to table eleven.
  2. Server: Table eleven has just questioned it. Their ticket shows a salad, not a tart.
  3. Runner: I'll tell the expediter exactly which dish and table were involved in the mix-up.
  4. Server: Please do. We need replacement instructions, not simply a guess about switching the plates back.
  5. Runner: Agreed. The tart has already reached the guest, so I'll explain that fact clearly.
  6. Server: I'll speak to table eleven and acknowledge the wrong dish while you check the replacement.
  7. Runner: The expediter confirms a fresh salad is being prepared, with a five-minute estimated wait.
  8. Server: I'll offer that estimate to the guest and ask whether the replacement still suits them.
  9. Runner: Do you need me to speak with table seven as well about their missing dish?
  10. Server: Yes, please explain that we're checking their order; don't claim it's already on its way.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.
