REFERENCE
Course: Restaurant Serving English
Professional audience: Restaurant servers, food runners, and dining-room service staff
Scope: All restaurants, prices, guests, and service events are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not food-safety certification, medical advice, or payment compliance. Follow current local law and actual allergy, food-handling, payment, and emergency procedures. Menu descriptions do not establish allergen safety. Do not delay urgent help for a routine service conversation.
Lesson 05: Correcting a dish without blame
Original fictional case: At table 3, guest Alex ordered vegetable pasta but received mushroom risotto. The ticket confirms vegetable pasta. Server Dana checks the mismatch and learns that the kitchen can remake the pasta with an estimated ten-minute wait. Dana may offer that replacement, but any bill adjustment requires shift lead review. The cause of the wrong delivery is not established. No allergy is reported. Alex has not accepted the remake at the start, and neither compensation nor a guaranteed ten-minute arrival has been approved.
Language workshop: Pasta ordered, risotto delivered
Communication goal: Acknowledge an order mismatch, offer a specific remedy, and separate replacement timing from a bill-adjustment decision.
Language guidance: The ticket confirms pasta, so the server can acknowledge the mismatch without guessing its cause. The kitchen offers a remake; the guest still needs to accept it. An available remedy is not the same as a completed resolution.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): You ordered vegetable pasta, and we brought mushroom risotto. / I am sorry; that is not the dish you ordered. / I checked the ticket, and it confirms the pasta. / You do not need to explain the order again. / The kitchen can remake the vegetable pasta. / The current estimate is ten minutes. / Would you like that replacement? / I cannot guarantee an exact arrival time. / I have not established how the mismatch happened. / I understand that the extra wait affects your meal. / Any bill adjustment needs the shift lead's review. / I cannot confirm a free item before approval. / I will bring the bill question to the shift lead. / I will wait for your go-ahead before arranging the replacement. / I will confirm that the correct dish reaches you. / I will check the bill question first, then come back for your decision.
Vocabulary: order mismatch: Difference between what was ordered and what was delivered or recorded. / remake: Newly prepared replacement for a dish that needs correction. / replacement offer: Proposed substitute or corrected item that the guest may accept. / service recovery: Actions addressing a service failure and its effect on the guest. / apology: Direct expression of regret for an error or inconvenience. / inconvenience: Practical difficulty or disruption caused to the guest. / ticket check: Review of the order record to establish what was entered. / delivered dish: Food actually brought to the guest. / ordered dish: Food the guest requested. / confirmed mismatch: Discrepancy established by the available record and observation. / cause: Reason an error occurred, which may require further investigation. / blame: Attribution of fault to a person or group. / remedy: Action intended to put a problem right. / estimated wait: Approximate expected delay before a specified next event. / acceptance: Agreement to a proposed option. / bill adjustment: Authorized change to an amount on the guest's bill. / comp: Informal restaurant term for an item provided without charge under authorization. / discount: Reduction from a stated price. / refund: Return of money already paid. / void: Cancellation of a transaction or entry through the relevant system procedure. / shift lead: Person coordinating service decisions during a shift. / approval limit: Boundary on the decisions someone may authorize. / resolution status: Whether the proposed correction has actually been agreed and completed. / follow-up check: Later confirmation that the corrective action occurred and met the stated need.
Speaking task: Check the cloze answers. Read the apology and offer with a partner, acknowledging the wrong dish without assigning blame. Switch roles. Keep Alex's go-ahead pending and the bill decision with the shift lead throughout. Complete the soup transfer and check its key. Repeat the dish names, estimated wait, and named lead accurately.
Partner: Alex: Guest at table 3 / Dana: Server
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Table 7 ordered lentil soup but received tomato soup. The ticket confirms lentil soup. A remake is available with an estimated seven-minute wait. Lead Noor must review any bill adjustment.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Alex: Thank you. Can the lead speak with me if there are questions? I would prefer to explain what the extra wait means for us.
Dana: I will pass that request to the shift lead, along with the ticket, what arrived, and the ten-minute estimate.
Alex: All right. For now, please check the bill question. Then I will decide whether to wait for the pasta.
Dana: That is our next step. I am sorry your meal has been interrupted. I will return with the decision rather than leave you wondering what is happening.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Explaining and splitting a billWhat does three ways mean?+

Your goal

Clarify equal payments versus item-based checks, state the arithmetic, and confirm the method before processing payment.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

What does three ways mean?

Three diners have a fictional $72 bill, with all listed charges included. Guest Morgan asks to split it "three ways." The restaurant can arrange equal payments or separate checks by item before payment is processed. Server Jules must ask which method the diners mean. Equal shares would be $24 each; item-based shares cannot be calculated without the item allocation. No additional charge, tip decision, completed payment, or refund is supplied. Morgan later confirms that the group wants three equal payments, and Jules reads back that choice before processing.

Morgan: Guest speaking for the group / Jules: Server

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the stated bill total?

Answer and explanation

A. $72 with all listed charges included

The scenario supplies a seventy-two-dollar total with its listed charges already included.

2. What does three ways need to clarify?

Answer and explanation

A. Equal payments or separate checks by item

The phrase can refer to different allocation methods even with three diners.

3. What is each equal share?

Answer and explanation

D. $24

Seventy-two divided by three equals twenty-four dollars per equal share.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Three equal shares of $72 are $24 each. That arithmetic does not show what each diner consumed. Separate checks by item need an agreed allocation, including shared items, before amounts can be determined.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
check
Restaurant bill listing the guest's charges.bring the check
bill total
Amount due for the listed charges.confirm the bill total
itemized bill
Bill showing individual items or charges.review the itemized bill
split payment
Payment of one amount through multiple payments or payers.arrange a split payment
equal share
Same portion of the total assigned to each person.calculate an equal share
separate check
Individual bill assigned to a particular diner or group.prepare separate checks
item allocation
Assignment of particular ordered items to particular payers.confirm the item allocation
shared item
Dish or charge used by more than one diner.allocate a shared item
allocation method
Agreed way of dividing the total or items.clarify the allocation method
subtotal
Sum before the specified additional components included in a final total.distinguish the subtotal
listed charge
Amount explicitly shown on the bill.verify a listed charge
service charge
Charge imposed under the restaurant's stated terms, distinct from a discretionary tip.explain a service charge
gratuity
Usually a tip; an automatic mandatory charge may instead be a service charge under applicable rules.clarify a gratuity
payment method
Means used to pay, such as a card or cash.confirm the payment method
card terminal
Device used to submit a card payment.present the card terminal
authorization
Approval stage for a payment under the relevant payment system.check payment authorization
payment receipt
Record acknowledging the payment transaction.provide a payment receipt
outstanding balance
Amount that remains unpaid.confirm the outstanding balance
partial payment
Payment of less than the full amount due.record a partial payment
payment confirmation
Evidence or acknowledgment that a payment was completed at the relevant stage.check payment confirmation
rounding
Adjustment to an available monetary precision under the relevant method.explain rounding
duplicate charge
Repeated charge for the same item or transaction where duplication is at issue.investigate a duplicate charge
reconciliation
Comparison of payments and amounts due to check that records agree.complete payment reconciliation
settle the bill
Complete payment of the amount due.settle the bill

The scenario supplies a total with all listed charges included; do not add a guessed fee or assume a tip decision. Confirm actual restaurant terms and the guest-selected payment amounts. Agreeing a split is not proof that money has been collected.

C Notice the language

Do not make the social decision for the table

Guests may use split loosely, while differing about who should pay for shared dishes. Clarify the method without judging fairness or announcing private assumptions about relationships. Keep the conversation practical and confirm amounts before a payment is submitted.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify the request
Do you mean three equal payments or separate checks by item?
State the total
The bill total is $72.
Explain equal shares
Three equal shares would be $24 each.
Explain the other method
For item-based checks, I need to know which items belong to each person.
Handle shared dishes
How should the shared items be allocated?
Avoid a fairness assumption
I will use the method your group agrees on.
Check the timing
We can arrange either method before processing payment.
Confirm the selected method
You would like three equal payments, correct?
Read back the amount
That is $24 for each of the three payments.
Do not invent an extra charge
The stated total already includes all listed charges.
Separate the tip decision
Would you like to decide on any optional tip separately?
Keep payment status factual
The split is agreed, but no payment has been processed yet.
Check the terminal amount
Please check the amount before confirming the payment.
Track what remains
We need to record each actual payment against the balance.
Offer a receipt
Would each person like their own payment receipt?
Close before processing
Three equal payments of $24, against the $72 total.
6 language and precision notes
Do you mean
Offers two concrete interpretations instead of making a guess.
Would be
Gives the amount conditional on choosing equal shares.
By item
Refers to consumption allocation rather than equal portions of the total.
Before processing
Locates the clarification before a transaction occurs.
Each
Makes clear that twenty-four dollars applies to every equal share.
Agreed, but not processed
Separates a payment plan from completed payment.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which first question resolves the ambiguity?

Answer and explanation

B. Do you mean equal payments or separate checks by item?

The question presents the two supported methods without guessing the group's intention.

2. Which arithmetic is correct for equal shares?

Answer and explanation

D. $72 divided by 3 is $24 each.

Three times twenty-four equals the full seventy-two-dollar total.

3. What is needed to calculate item-based checks?

Answer and explanation

A. An agreed allocation of items and shared charges

Item-based amounts depend on who is assigned each charge, not simply the diner count.

4. What does confirmation of three equal payments establish?

Answer and explanation

D. The intended allocation, not completed payment

Agreeing the method precedes processing and does not prove that any payment has been completed.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

What does three ways mean?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Morgan: Guest speaking for the group / Jules: Server

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • card terminal
  • payment confirmation
  • split payment
  • listed charge
  • payment receipt
  • equal shares
  • allocation method
  • outstanding balance
  • item allocation
  • bill total
  1. Morgan
    Could we split this three ways, please? There are three of us, and we would like to sort it out before everyone starts reaching for a card.
  2. Jules
    Certainly. Do you mean three 1, or would you like separate checks according to what each person ordered? We can arrange either before payment.
  3. Morgan
    What would it come to if we divided it equally? We shared a couple of dishes, so that may be easiest.
  4. Jules
    The 2 is seventy-two dollars. Divided equally among three people, that is twenty-four dollars each. Those three amounts add back to seventy-two.
  5. Morgan
    If someone wants to pay only for their own meal, can you do that too? We would need to sort out the shared starters.
  6. Jules
    Yes. That requires an 3. We would also need your agreement about shared items rather than assume an equal division of those.
  7. Morgan
    We did share some food, so equal payments may be simpler for us. Let me confirm with the others before you enter anything.
  8. Jules
    Of course. The 4 should be your group's choice. I will wait rather than treat our discussion as permission to process a payment.
  9. Morgan
    They have agreed. We want three equal payments, not separate bills based on individual dishes. Twenty-four each sounds right to us.
  10. Jules
    Thank you. I will arrange the 5 as three payments of twenty-four dollars against the seventy-two-dollar total. Please confirm that is the method you want.
  11. Morgan
    Yes, that is correct. I want to make sure the number on the terminal will match that amount, not the whole seventy-two dollars.
  12. Jules
    We will check each amount on the 6 before it is confirmed. Agreeing twenty-four each does not mean a payment has already been taken.
  13. Morgan
    Does the seventy-two already include everything listed on this check? I just want to be sure we are dividing the final figure.
  14. Jules
    Correct. The 7 amounts are included in the stated total. I will not invent an additional fee or assume a tip choice from this request.
  15. Morgan
    Could we each have a receipt for our own payment? Two of us need one for our records.
  16. Jules
    We can provide the relevant 8 through our payment process. It needs to reflect the payment that is actually completed, not just the split we have agreed.
  17. Morgan
    We will pay one at a time, then. Could you show each person the amount before they tap?
  18. Jules
    Yes. The 9 must reflect the actual payments recorded. Three agreed amounts are not a substitute for checking which transactions have completed.
  19. Morgan
    Yes, twenty-four each. We are all agreed now. You can bring the terminal when you are ready.
  20. Jules
    Exactly. We will obtain 10 through the actual process. Three equal payments of twenty-four dollars is the instruction I am working from.

Answers and explanations
  1. equal shares Turn 2. Equal shares divide the total into the same amount for each diner, unlike separate checks based on individual items.
  2. bill total Turn 4. The bill total is the stated seventy-two dollars, including all listed charges in this fictional scenario.
  3. item allocation Turn 6. Item allocation assigns charges to the relevant diners; their amounts cannot be calculated from the diner count alone.
  4. allocation method Turn 8. The allocation method is the group's agreed way of dividing the bill, which the server should not choose for them.
  5. split payment Turn 10. The split payment applies three agreed twenty-four-dollar payments to one seventy-two-dollar bill; it does not change the total.
  6. card terminal Turn 12. The card terminal displays the payment being submitted; checking its amount helps prevent an incorrect full-bill charge.
  7. listed charge Turn 14. A listed charge is already part of the supplied total; splitting the payment does not justify charging it a second time.
  8. payment receipt Turn 16. A payment receipt records an actual transaction; it should not be represented as proof of payment before processing occurs.
  9. outstanding balance Turn 18. The outstanding balance is what remains unpaid after actual recorded payments, not an amount inferred solely from the agreed split.
  10. payment confirmation Turn 20. Payment confirmation establishes the relevant completed transaction status, which remains separate from agreeing a payment plan.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Morgan: Could we split this three ways, please? There are three of us, and we would like to sort it out before everyone starts reaching for a card.
  2. Jules: Certainly. Do you mean three equal shares, or would you like separate checks according to what each person ordered? We can arrange either before payment.
  3. Morgan: What would it come to if we divided it equally? We shared a couple of dishes, so that may be easiest.
  4. Jules: The bill total is seventy-two dollars. Divided equally among three people, that is twenty-four dollars each. Those three amounts add back to seventy-two.
  5. Morgan: If someone wants to pay only for their own meal, can you do that too? We would need to sort out the shared starters.
  6. Jules: Yes. That requires an item allocation. We would also need your agreement about shared items rather than assume an equal division of those.
  7. Morgan: We did share some food, so equal payments may be simpler for us. Let me confirm with the others before you enter anything.
  8. Jules: Of course. The allocation method should be your group's choice. I will wait rather than treat our discussion as permission to process a payment.
  9. Morgan: They have agreed. We want three equal payments, not separate bills based on individual dishes. Twenty-four each sounds right to us.
  10. Jules: Thank you. I will arrange the split payment as three payments of twenty-four dollars against the seventy-two-dollar total. Please confirm that is the method you want.
  11. Morgan: Yes, that is correct. I want to make sure the number on the terminal will match that amount, not the whole seventy-two dollars.
  12. Jules: We will check each amount on the card terminal before it is confirmed. Agreeing twenty-four each does not mean a payment has already been taken.
  13. Morgan: Does the seventy-two already include everything listed on this check? I just want to be sure we are dividing the final figure.
  14. Jules: Correct. The listed charge amounts are included in the stated total. I will not invent an additional fee or assume a tip choice from this request.
  15. Morgan: Could we each have a receipt for our own payment? Two of us need one for our records.
  16. Jules: We can provide the relevant payment receipt through our payment process. It needs to reflect the payment that is actually completed, not just the split we have agreed.
  17. Morgan: We will pay one at a time, then. Could you show each person the amount before they tap?
  18. Jules: Yes. The outstanding balance must reflect the actual payments recorded. Three agreed amounts are not a substitute for checking which transactions have completed.
  19. Morgan: Yes, twenty-four each. We are all agreed now. You can bring the terminal when you are ready.
  20. Jules: Exactly. We will obtain payment confirmation through the actual process. Three equal payments of twenty-four dollars is the instruction I am working from.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Three ways can mean two things

The $72 bill in the case is unpaid.

  1. Server: Before arranging a split payment, would you like equal shares or separate checks by item?
  2. Guest: Actually, we'd each like to pay for our own food rather than divide everything equally.
  3. Server: Certainly. Equal thirds would be twenty-four dollars, but I'll prepare separate checks by item instead.
  4. Guest: Thank you. I had the pasta and water, and my colleague here had the tart.
  5. Server: Who should have the shared dessert on their check, or would you like it divided?
  6. Guest: Put the whole dessert on mine, please. I offered to treat them to that part.
  7. Server: I'll allocate the dessert to your check, and confirm the remaining drink with the third diner.
  8. Guest: That drink belongs to them. Can we see the individual bills before making any payments?
  9. Server: Yes. I'll bring the three checks back for review before asking you to pay.
  10. Guest: Great. We meant three separate bills, so I'm glad you checked before assuming equal amounts.
A charge appears twice

A guest queries a duplicate drink on an unpaid bill.

  1. Guest: The bill shows two lemonades, but I only ordered one during the whole meal.
  2. Server: Let me check the order record and the two entries before we process your payment.
  3. Guest: One line says lemonade and the other says the same thing with a later time.
  4. Server: I can see both entries. Did anyone else at your table order a second one?
  5. Guest: No. My companion had water, and there was no refill or second glass for me.
  6. Server: Thank you. The shift lead has confirmed the duplicate entry and approved its removal.
  7. Guest: Could you bring the revised bill so I can see which amount actually changed?
  8. Server: Certainly. This corrected version has one lemonade; the duplicate line is no longer charged.
  9. Guest: That matches what we had. Is this now the version we'll use for payment?
  10. Server: Yes, this revised bill is the one to review and pay, not the earlier copy.
An optional extra is not required

A server explains a fictional bill with a labeled service charge.

  1. Guest: This bill has a service charge and another blank line. Are both amounts required?
  2. Server: The printed service charge is included in this total; the blank line is for an optional tip.
  3. Guest: So leaving that optional line blank doesn't remove the service charge already printed above it?
  4. Server: Correct. They are different entries. The total here already includes the restaurant's stated service charge.
  5. Guest: Can you tell me how that charge is distributed among the people working tonight?
  6. Server: I don't have a verified explanation of distribution. Our shift lead can answer that question.
  7. Guest: Please ask them. I want to understand the charge before deciding about an extra tip.
  8. Server: I'll bring them over, and leave the payment unprocessed while you discuss that question.
  9. Guest: Thank you. Please don't infer any optional tip amount from what we paid last time.
  10. Server: Of course. Any optional extra is your choice, not an assumption from a previous visit.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

What does three ways mean?
  1. Check the answers. Read the exchange, contrasting equal payments with separate checks by item.
  2. Switch roles. Say three payments of twenty-four against seventy-two, then confirm that agreement is not completed payment.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Clarify four ways

Four diners have a $96 total with all listed charges included. The restaurant supports equal payments or item-based checks before payment. The diners confirm equal payments. No payment has been processed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Server: "You have chosen ___ payments."

Answer and explanation

C. equal

The diners explicitly choose equal payments rather than item-based checks.

2. Guest: "Each share is ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

D. twenty-four

Ninety-six divided by four equals twenty-four dollars per diner.

3. Server: "The full total remains ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

B. ninety-six

Changing the allocation does not change the stated ninety-six-dollar total.

4. Guest: "The payments are not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

B. processed

The scenario confirms the method but supplies no completed payment.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Restaurant Serving English
Professional audience: Restaurant servers, food runners, and dining-room service staff
Scope: All restaurants, prices, guests, and service events are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not food-safety certification, medical advice, or payment compliance. Follow current local law and actual allergy, food-handling, payment, and emergency procedures. Menu descriptions do not establish allergen safety. Do not delay urgent help for a routine service conversation.
Lesson 06: Explaining and splitting a bill
Original fictional case: Three diners have a fictional $72 bill, with all listed charges included. Guest Morgan asks to split it "three ways." The restaurant can arrange equal payments or separate checks by item before payment is processed. Server Jules must ask which method the diners mean. Equal shares would be $24 each; item-based shares cannot be calculated without the item allocation. No additional charge, tip decision, completed payment, or refund is supplied. Morgan later confirms that the group wants three equal payments, and Jules reads back that choice before processing.
Language workshop: What does three ways mean?
Communication goal: Clarify equal payments versus item-based checks, state the arithmetic, and confirm the method before processing payment.
Language guidance: Three equal shares of $72 are $24 each. That arithmetic does not show what each diner consumed. Separate checks by item need an agreed allocation, including shared items, before amounts can be determined.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Do you mean three equal payments or separate checks by item? / The bill total is $72. / Three equal shares would be $24 each. / For item-based checks, I need to know which items belong to each person. / How should the shared items be allocated? / I will use the method your group agrees on. / We can arrange either method before processing payment. / You would like three equal payments, correct? / That is $24 for each of the three payments. / The stated total already includes all listed charges. / Would you like to decide on any optional tip separately? / The split is agreed, but no payment has been processed yet. / Please check the amount before confirming the payment. / We need to record each actual payment against the balance. / Would each person like their own payment receipt? / Three equal payments of $24, against the $72 total.
Vocabulary: check: Restaurant bill listing the guest's charges. / bill total: Amount due for the listed charges. / itemized bill: Bill showing individual items or charges. / split payment: Payment of one amount through multiple payments or payers. / equal share: Same portion of the total assigned to each person. / separate check: Individual bill assigned to a particular diner or group. / item allocation: Assignment of particular ordered items to particular payers. / shared item: Dish or charge used by more than one diner. / allocation method: Agreed way of dividing the total or items. / subtotal: Sum before the specified additional components included in a final total. / listed charge: Amount explicitly shown on the bill. / service charge: Charge imposed under the restaurant's stated terms, distinct from a discretionary tip. / gratuity: Usually a tip; an automatic mandatory charge may instead be a service charge under applicable rules. / payment method: Means used to pay, such as a card or cash. / card terminal: Device used to submit a card payment. / authorization: Approval stage for a payment under the relevant payment system. / payment receipt: Record acknowledging the payment transaction. / outstanding balance: Amount that remains unpaid. / partial payment: Payment of less than the full amount due. / payment confirmation: Evidence or acknowledgment that a payment was completed at the relevant stage. / rounding: Adjustment to an available monetary precision under the relevant method. / duplicate charge: Repeated charge for the same item or transaction where duplication is at issue. / reconciliation: Comparison of payments and amounts due to check that records agree. / settle the bill: Complete payment of the amount due.
Speaking task: Check the answers. Read the exchange, contrasting equal payments with separate checks by item. Switch roles. Say three payments of twenty-four against seventy-two, then confirm that agreement is not completed payment. Complete and check the ninety-six-dollar transfer. Read four equal shares of twenty-four without changing the total.
Partner: Morgan: Guest speaking for the group / Jules: Server
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Four diners have a $96 total with all listed charges included. The restaurant supports equal payments or item-based checks before payment. The diners confirm equal payments. No payment has been processed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Morgan: We will pay one at a time, then. Could you show each person the amount before they tap?
Jules: Yes. The outstanding balance must reflect the actual payments recorded. Three agreed amounts are not a substitute for checking which transactions have completed.
Morgan: Yes, twenty-four each. We are all agreed now. You can bring the terminal when you are ready.
Jules: Exactly. We will obtain payment confirmation through the actual process. Three equal payments of twenty-four dollars is the instruction I am working from.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Agreeing on clearing and shared serviceAn empty plate still in use+

Your goal

Check permission to clear specific items and coordinate shared service without inferring that an empty plate is finished.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

An empty plate still in use

At table 2, one diner has an empty plate, but the shared vegetable dish still contains food. The diner is using that plate for another portion. Server Mina and food runner Leo need to coordinate clearing. Extra sharing plates can be offered, but active items should remain unless the guest agrees otherwise. No request to end the meal or prepare the table for new guests has been made. The colleagues discuss the words Leo can use, then agree that he will ask what can be cleared rather than silently remove the plate.

Mina: Server responsible for table 2 / Leo: Food runner helping with clearing

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Why is the empty plate still needed?

Answer and explanation

C. The diner is using it for another portion.

The brief states that the diner will use the plate for more shared food.

2. What remains in the shared dish?

Answer and explanation

B. Vegetables

The shared vegetable dish still contains food in this case.

3. What may the team offer?

Answer and explanation

B. Extra sharing plates

Extra sharing plates are available to offer, while clearing still needs clarification.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Empty describes the plate contents at one moment. Finished describes the guest no longer needing it. The two are not equivalent here. A shared dish with food and an empty individual plate can belong to the same continuing meal.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
clear
Remove dining items that are ready to be taken away.ask before clearing
pre-bus
Remove agreed finished items during a meal before the table's final clearing.pre-bus discreetly
busser
Team member who supports clearing, resetting, and related dining-room tasks.coordinate with the busser
food runner
Colleague who carries prepared dishes to guests and may assist service.brief the food runner
sharing plate
Plate provided for taking a portion from a shared dish.offer a sharing plate
shared dish
Food presented for more than one person to serve from.leave the shared dish
serving utensil
Tool used to transfer food from a serving dish to a plate.provide a serving utensil
place setting
Arrangement of dining items for one person.maintain the place setting
side plate
Small plate used for bread, sides, or another specified purpose.offer a side plate
sideboard
Service furniture or station used to hold dining-room supplies.restock the sideboard
service station
Area holding supplies and equipment for dining-room work.organize the service station
cutlery
Knives, forks, and spoons used for eating.replace the cutlery
flatware
Common US term for eating utensils such as knives, forks, and spoons.provide clean flatware
crockery
Plates, bowls, and other ceramic dining vessels.handle the crockery
glassware
Drinking vessels made of glass.collect finished glassware
active item
Item still being used or needed by a guest.leave active items
finished item
Item the guest has finished using and agrees can be removed.identify finished items
clearing permission
Guest agreement to remove a particular item.obtain clearing permission
service cue
Action or signal that may suggest a service need but can require clarification.clarify a service cue
meal pace
Speed and rhythm at which guests are eating.respect the meal pace
table reset
Preparation of a cleared table for its next guests.begin an authorized table reset
table turnover
Transition from one party's use of a table to the next.manage table turnover
unobtrusive service
Assistance that meets guest needs without unnecessary interruption.provide unobtrusive service
guest preference
Individual request about how service should be provided.confirm the guest preference

May I clear this plate? asks about a particular item. Are you finished with everything? is broader and can create pressure or misunderstanding. Identify the item, wait for the answer, and keep the agreed clearing action aligned across the team.

C Notice the language

Ask rather than apply a dining stereotype

Dining customs differ, but nationality is not a reliable instruction for a particular table. An empty plate, a pause, or a cutlery position can be ambiguous. Ask a short question about the actual item and respect the answer without making guests explain their culture.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Ask about an item
May I clear this plate, or are you still using it?
Accept continued use
Of course; I will leave it for your next portion.
Check the shared dish
Would you like the vegetables to stay on the table?
Offer practical help
Would extra sharing plates be useful?
Avoid an assumption
An empty plate does not necessarily mean the guest has finished.
Coordinate the team
Please leave that plate; it is still in use.
State the observed fact
The shared dish still has food in it.
Keep the question narrow
Ask about the specific item rather than the whole meal.
Wait for permission
Let the guest answer before reaching for the plate.
Respect a refusal
Certainly; I will leave it where it is.
Avoid cultural guesses
We should ask this table what they prefer.
Separate stages
Clearing one finished item is not a table reset.
Preserve the shared arrangement
Leave the serving dish and active plates in place.
Keep extra items optional
We can offer more plates without adding them automatically.
Pass on the preference
The diner is keeping the plate for another portion.
Close the service loop
Confirm what may be cleared and tell the colleague helping you.
6 language and precision notes
This plate
Makes the permission request specific.
Or are you still using it
Gives a comfortable way to decline clearing.
Of course
Accepts the preference without making it an inconvenience.
Still
Signals continued use despite the empty surface.
Would be useful
Offers help instead of imposing equipment.
Not necessarily
Challenges an assumption without saying the cue is never meaningful.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which question is most appropriate?

Answer and explanation

D. May I clear this plate, or are you still using it?

The question checks the actual plate and allows the diner to keep using it.

2. What does the empty plate establish?

Answer and explanation

B. Its current contents, not that it is no longer needed

The plate is empty but remains in use for another portion.

3. Which instruction should Mina give Leo?

Answer and explanation

C. Leave the active plate and ask what may be cleared.

The instruction preserves active items while allowing a specific clearing-permission check.

4. How should cultural differences be handled?

Answer and explanation

D. Ask the individual table about its preference.

A direct, respectful question establishes the actual preference without relying on group stereotypes.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

An empty plate still in use

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mina: Server responsible for table 2 / Leo: Food runner helping with clearing

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • table reset
  • guest preference
  • sharing plate
  • Unobtrusive service
  • clearing permission
  • meal pace
  • active item
  • finished item
  • service cue
  • shared dish
  1. Leo
    Mina, one plate at table two is empty. I was about to take it away, but the vegetable dish in the middle still has food.
  2. Mina
    Please leave that 1 for now. The diner is using the plate for another portion, so empty does not mean finished in this situation.
  3. Leo
    I see. They are sharing, so the empty plate is not finished with. I will leave it where it is.
  4. Mina
    A 2 can help us notice a need, but it is not always permission. We should check the actual item before reaching across the table.
  5. Leo
    What would you say without interrupting the conversation too much? I want the question to be clear but not sound as though I am trying to hurry them.
  6. Mina
    Ask for 3 briefly: May I clear this plate, or are you still using it? Then pause long enough for an answer.
  7. Leo
    If they say they are using it for more vegetables, I can simply say of course and leave it. I do not need them to justify it.
  8. Mina
    Exactly. Respect the 4. We should not turn a small service question into a discussion of their dining customs or why they want to keep the plate.
  9. Leo
    We can offer extra plates as well, correct? That might make sharing easier, but I should not bring a stack of them without asking.
  10. Mina
    Yes, offer a 5 or more if useful. Say, Would extra sharing plates help? We have that option available, but it should remain their choice.
  11. Leo
    I will keep the vegetables in place too. There is still food there, and no one has asked us to remove the dish.
  12. Mina
    Good. The 6 and the individual plate belong to the same ongoing meal. Do not treat them as unrelated clearing decisions.
  13. Leo
    If another guest has actually finished with a glass or plate, may I check that item separately? I do not want to leave everything unnecessarily.
  14. Mina
    Yes. A 7 can be handled separately. Ask specifically, and keep the action narrow enough that we do not accidentally clear something another diner is using.
  15. Leo
    Right. I will check the glass separately, not start taking the whole setting away while they still have food.
  16. Mina
    Correct. A 8 is another stage. Our immediate task is to support this table's meal, not turn an empty plate into a deadline.
  17. Leo
    I will ask the diner, leave the active items, and offer extra sharing plates. Then I will tell you what they want so we stay consistent.
  18. Mina
    Thank you. That supports their 9. It also prevents a second colleague from returning a moment later and asking to take the same plate.
  19. Leo
    I will keep it brief at the table. May I clear this plate, or are you still using it? Then I wait for the answer.
  20. Mina
    Exactly. 10 means noticing, asking briefly, and following the answer. Clear what is agreed, leave what is active, and keep the team informed.

Answers and explanations
  1. active item Turn 2. The active item is the empty plate still needed for another portion; its empty surface does not make it finished.
  2. service cue Turn 4. A service cue may suggest a need, but the empty plate is ambiguous and requires checking with the guest.
  3. clearing permission Turn 6. Clearing permission is the guest's agreement to remove the specific item, which should be obtained before taking it.
  4. guest preference Turn 8. The guest preference is the actual table's request, not a rule inferred from nationality or an empty plate.
  5. sharing plate Turn 10. A sharing plate can help guests take portions from a shared dish, but the extra item remains an offer.
  6. shared dish Turn 12. The shared dish still contains vegetables for continued service, so one empty individual plate does not establish permission to remove it.
  7. finished item Turn 14. A finished item can be removed with appropriate agreement without treating the entire table as finished with the meal.
  8. table reset Turn 16. A table reset prepares the setting for new guests; clearing one agreed item during a meal is a different stage.
  9. meal pace Turn 18. Meal pace is the guests' eating rhythm, which the team can respect by checking needs instead of rushing active items away.
  10. Unobtrusive service Turn 20. Unobtrusive service meets the real need with minimal disruption, combining a specific question with respectful follow-through.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Leo: Mina, one plate at table two is empty. I was about to take it away, but the vegetable dish in the middle still has food.
  2. Mina: Please leave that active item for now. The diner is using the plate for another portion, so empty does not mean finished in this situation.
  3. Leo: I see. They are sharing, so the empty plate is not finished with. I will leave it where it is.
  4. Mina: A service cue can help us notice a need, but it is not always permission. We should check the actual item before reaching across the table.
  5. Leo: What would you say without interrupting the conversation too much? I want the question to be clear but not sound as though I am trying to hurry them.
  6. Mina: Ask for clearing permission briefly: May I clear this plate, or are you still using it? Then pause long enough for an answer.
  7. Leo: If they say they are using it for more vegetables, I can simply say of course and leave it. I do not need them to justify it.
  8. Mina: Exactly. Respect the guest preference. We should not turn a small service question into a discussion of their dining customs or why they want to keep the plate.
  9. Leo: We can offer extra plates as well, correct? That might make sharing easier, but I should not bring a stack of them without asking.
  10. Mina: Yes, offer a sharing plate or more if useful. Say, Would extra sharing plates help? We have that option available, but it should remain their choice.
  11. Leo: I will keep the vegetables in place too. There is still food there, and no one has asked us to remove the dish.
  12. Mina: Good. The shared dish and the individual plate belong to the same ongoing meal. Do not treat them as unrelated clearing decisions.
  13. Leo: If another guest has actually finished with a glass or plate, may I check that item separately? I do not want to leave everything unnecessarily.
  14. Mina: Yes. A finished item can be handled separately. Ask specifically, and keep the action narrow enough that we do not accidentally clear something another diner is using.
  15. Leo: Right. I will check the glass separately, not start taking the whole setting away while they still have food.
  16. Mina: Correct. A table reset is another stage. Our immediate task is to support this table's meal, not turn an empty plate into a deadline.
  17. Leo: I will ask the diner, leave the active items, and offer extra sharing plates. Then I will tell you what they want so we stay consistent.
  18. Mina: Thank you. That supports their meal pace. It also prevents a second colleague from returning a moment later and asking to take the same plate.
  19. Leo: I will keep it brief at the table. May I clear this plate, or are you still using it? Then I wait for the answer.
  20. Mina: Exactly. Unobtrusive service means noticing, asking briefly, and following the answer. Clear what is agreed, leave what is active, and keep the team informed.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

An empty plate still has a purpose

Table 2 has a shared vegetable dish, as in the case.

  1. Server: May I clear this plate, or are you still using it for the shared vegetables?
  2. Guest: Please leave it. I was just about to take another portion from that serving dish.
  3. Server: Of course. Are these two empty glasses finished with, or would you prefer them left too?
  4. Guest: You can take the glasses. We'd like to keep the water jug and our water glasses.
  5. Server: I'll clear only the two empty juice glasses, leaving the jug and all plates here.
  6. Guest: Thank you. Could we have another sharing plate for the person at the end?
  7. Server: Certainly. Would one small plate be enough, or does anyone else need one as well?
  8. Guest: Just one, please. The rest of us already have plates for the shared food.
  9. Server: Here you are. I'll leave the serving dish until you tell me you're finished with it.
  10. Guest: That's helpful. We're taking our time, but we don't need all those empty glasses.
Reaching across a conversation

A server needs permission to retrieve a dish near a guest.

  1. Server: Excuse me, may I reach across for that empty dish, or should I come around?
  2. Guest: Please come around. There's an open laptop here, and I don't want anything moved across it.
  3. Server: Certainly. I'll approach from the other side so your laptop can stay where it is.
  4. Guest: Thank you. The dish is finished, but the cup beside it still has tea inside.
  5. Server: I'll take just the dish. Would you like the cup moved closer to you?
  6. Guest: No, it's fine there. I'm waiting for the tea to cool before I drink it.
  7. Server: Understood. I'll leave the cup and its saucer exactly where you've placed them.
  8. Guest: Could you remove the unused menu too? We have everything we want for now.
  9. Server: Yes, I'll take that menu from this side without disturbing the laptop or drink.
  10. Guest: That gives us more room. Thanks for checking which things we still needed.
Enough settings for a shared dessert

Two diners want one dessert but separate spoons and plates.

  1. Guest: We're sharing one dessert. Could you bring enough tableware so we don't have to pass it around?
  2. Server: Certainly. Would you like two small plates and a spoon for each of you?
  3. Guest: Yes, please. The dessert can stay in the center while we divide it ourselves.
  4. Server: I'll bring two dessert place settings and leave room for the shared dish.
  5. Guest: Please leave our coffee cups as well, even though one looks almost empty now.
  6. Server: Of course. Are you finished with the bread basket, or is that still in use?
  7. Guest: The basket can go, but my friend is keeping the last piece on their side plate.
  8. Server: I'll remove the basket only. Your side plates and coffee cups will stay here.
  9. Guest: That sounds right. There should be plenty of space once the basket is gone.
  10. Server: Here are the dessert plates and spoons; I'll set the shared dish between you.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

An empty plate still in use
  1. Check the answers. Read Mina and Leo's exchange, then repeat the exact plate-clearing question in turn.
  2. Switch roles. Pause after the permission question; distinguish an empty plate from a plate no longer needed.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Check a shared dessert plate

At table 9, an empty plate is still needed for more shared dessert. The dessert dish contains food. Extra small plates can be offered. No permission to clear the active plate has been given.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Server: "The empty plate is still ___."

Answer and explanation

B. needed

The diner needs the plate for another portion of shared dessert.

2. Colleague: "The shared dish contains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. dessert

The remaining food in the supplied shared dish is dessert.

3. Server: "Extra small plates can be ___."

Answer and explanation

A. offered

The plates are an available offer, not an automatic addition.

4. Colleague: "Clearing permission has not been ___."

Answer and explanation

B. given

The scenario supplies no guest permission to remove the active plate.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary

3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 07: Agreeing on clearing and shared service
Original fictional case: At table 2, one diner has an empty plate, but the shared vegetable dish still contains food. The diner is using that plate for another portion. Server Mina and food runner Leo need to coordinate clearing. Extra sharing plates can be offered, but active items should remain unless the guest agrees otherwise. No request to end the meal or prepare the table for new guests has been made. The colleagues discuss the words Leo can use, then agree that he will ask what can be cleared rather than silently remove the plate.
Language workshop: An empty plate still in use
Communication goal: Check permission to clear specific items and coordinate shared service without inferring that an empty plate is finished.
Language guidance: Empty describes the plate contents at one moment. Finished describes the guest no longer needing it. The two are not equivalent here. A shared dish with food and an empty individual plate can belong to the same continuing meal.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): May I clear this plate, or are you still using it? / Of course; I will leave it for your next portion. / Would you like the vegetables to stay on the table? / Would extra sharing plates be useful? / An empty plate does not necessarily mean the guest has finished. / Please leave that plate; it is still in use. / The shared dish still has food in it. / Ask about the specific item rather than the whole meal. / Let the guest answer before reaching for the plate. / Certainly; I will leave it where it is. / We should ask this table what they prefer. / Clearing one finished item is not a table reset. / Leave the serving dish and active plates in place. / We can offer more plates without adding them automatically. / The diner is keeping the plate for another portion. / Confirm what may be cleared and tell the colleague helping you.
Vocabulary: clear: Remove dining items that are ready to be taken away. / pre-bus: Remove agreed finished items during a meal before the table's final clearing. / busser: Team member who supports clearing, resetting, and related dining-room tasks. / food runner: Colleague who carries prepared dishes to guests and may assist service. / sharing plate: Plate provided for taking a portion from a shared dish. / shared dish: Food presented for more than one person to serve from. / serving utensil: Tool used to transfer food from a serving dish to a plate. / place setting: Arrangement of dining items for one person. / side plate: Small plate used for bread, sides, or another specified purpose. / sideboard: Service furniture or station used to hold dining-room supplies. / service station: Area holding supplies and equipment for dining-room work. / cutlery: Knives, forks, and spoons used for eating. / flatware: Common US term for eating utensils such as knives, forks, and spoons. / crockery: Plates, bowls, and other ceramic dining vessels. / glassware: Drinking vessels made of glass. / active item: Item still being used or needed by a guest. / finished item: Item the guest has finished using and agrees can be removed. / clearing permission: Guest agreement to remove a particular item. / service cue: Action or signal that may suggest a service need but can require clarification. / meal pace: Speed and rhythm at which guests are eating. / table reset: Preparation of a cleared table for its next guests. / table turnover: Transition from one party's use of a table to the next. / unobtrusive service: Assistance that meets guest needs without unnecessary interruption. / guest preference: Individual request about how service should be provided.
Speaking task: Check the answers. Read Mina and Leo's exchange, then repeat the exact plate-clearing question in turn. Switch roles. Pause after the permission question; distinguish an empty plate from a plate no longer needed. Complete and check the dessert transfer. Repeat the offer of extra plates without implying clearing permission.
Partner: Mina: Server responsible for table 2 / Leo: Food runner helping with clearing
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: At table 9, an empty plate is still needed for more shared dessert. The dessert dish contains food. Extra small plates can be offered. No permission to clear the active plate has been given.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Leo: I will ask the diner, leave the active items, and offer extra sharing plates. Then I will tell you what they want so we stay consistent.
Mina: Thank you. That supports their meal pace. It also prevents a second colleague from returning a moment later and asking to take the same plate.
Leo: I will keep it brief at the table. May I clear this plate, or are you still using it? Then I wait for the answer.
Mina: Exactly. Unobtrusive service means noticing, asking briefly, and following the answer. Clear what is agreed, leave what is active, and keep the team informed.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Handing over a live sectionTwo tables, two unfinished tasks+

Your goal

Give a concise table-by-table handover that separates meal stage, payment status, and an unresolved charge query.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Two tables, two unfinished tasks

At 8:00, outgoing server Ben hands tables 4 and 5 to Maya. Table 4 has received its mains, but dessert has not been ordered. Table 5 has an open check and a pending query about a possible duplicate coffee charge. The shift lead is reviewing that query; no duplicate has yet been confirmed and no adjustment is approved. Ben needs Maya to accept both follow-ups explicitly. The handover does not establish a dessert order, a settled bill, or a resolved coffee issue. Maya agrees to take responsibility for the next guest-facing steps.

Ben: Outgoing server / Maya: Incoming server

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is table 4's meal stage?

Answer and explanation

C. Mains served; dessert not ordered

The brief states that the mains are served while dessert remains unchosen.

2. What is table 5's check status?

Answer and explanation

B. Open

Table 5 has an open check while the coffee query remains under review.

3. Who is reviewing the possible duplicate coffee charge?

Answer and explanation

C. The shift lead

The shift lead is already reviewing the query, but no result is supplied.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Mains served does not imply dessert ordered. Open check does not imply the bill is correct or the disputed item is wrong. A possible duplicate charge must remain a query until the review establishes the result.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
section
Group of tables assigned to a server or service team.take over a section
live table
Table still receiving service or awaiting completion of a task.identify live tables
outgoing server
Colleague transferring responsibility at a shift or section change.brief the outgoing server
incoming server
Colleague accepting responsibility for the next service period.confirm the incoming server
handover
Transfer of relevant information and responsibility between colleagues.complete the section handover
handover time
Time at which the responsibility transfer is recorded or agreed.state the handover time
table status
Current service stage and relevant conditions for a table.summarize the table status
mains served
Status indicating main dishes have reached the guests.confirm mains served
dessert decision
Guest choice about whether and what dessert to order.follow up the dessert decision
open check
Bill that has not been settled and closed in the relevant system.monitor the open check
pending query
Question awaiting an answer or decision.carry forward a pending query
suspected duplicate
Possible repeated charge that still requires verification.review a suspected duplicate
charge review
Examination of whether a billed amount is correct.request a charge review
decision owner
Person authorized or responsible for resolving a specified question.identify the decision owner
service owner
Person responsible for the guest-facing follow-up.name the service owner
accepted follow-up
Next action explicitly taken on by the receiving colleague.record an accepted follow-up
read-back confirmation
Repetition demonstrating that the receiving colleague understood the important details.obtain read-back confirmation
closed check
Bill marked settled and closed through the actual payment process.verify a closed check
status change
Actual transition from one confirmed state to another.communicate a status change
exception note
Record highlighting an issue outside routine service flow.preserve an exception note
unresolved item
Task or question that has not been completed or answered.track the unresolved item
continuity of service
Uninterrupted attention to guest needs across staff changes.maintain continuity of service
handover gap
Missing information or ownership during a transfer of responsibility.prevent a handover gap
follow-through
Completion of an accepted action and appropriate communication of its result.ensure follow-through

The shift lead owns the charge decision; Maya can own the guest-facing update and follow-up. Explicit acceptance keeps these roles connected. Do not close the issue merely because the outgoing server has finished a shift.

C Notice the language

A handover is an accepted responsibility

A rushed list of table numbers can hide unfinished work. State the meal stage, the open request, and the person handling any decision. Ask the receiving colleague to repeat the important details so that moving between shifts does not make a live guest concern disappear.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Open the transfer
It is 8:00, and I am handing over tables 4 and 5.
State table 4's stage
Table 4 has its mains.
Keep dessert accurate
Dessert has not been ordered.
Name the first follow-up
Please check whether table 4 wants dessert.
State table 5's balance status
Table 5 has an open check.
Identify the query
They have asked about a possible duplicate coffee charge.
Preserve uncertainty
The duplication has not been confirmed.
Name the decision owner
The shift lead is reviewing the charge query.
Avoid false compensation
No adjustment has been approved yet.
Ask for acceptance
Can you take both follow-ups?
Read back table 4
I will follow up dessert without treating it as already ordered.
Read back table 5
I will track the coffee review and update the guests.
Keep payment separate
The check remains open until the actual payment process is completed.
Preserve the record
Keep the unresolved query visible in the handover notes.
Avoid a false closure
The issue is not resolved just because the section changes hands.
Close with ownership
Maya has accepted both guest-facing follow-ups; the shift lead retains the charge review.
6 language and precision notes
Has its mains
Describes delivery, not the next course choice.
Has not been ordered
Prevents a future possibility from becoming a recorded order.
Possible duplicate
Preserves the uncertainty under review.
Is reviewing
Marks an active process, not a completed decision.
Can you take
Requests explicit ownership rather than assuming it.
Remains open
Keeps the payment state tied to actual events.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which handover for table 4 is correct?

Answer and explanation

C. Mains served; dessert not ordered; follow up the dessert choice.

The message preserves the known meal stage and identifies the unfinished dessert follow-up.

2. Which coffee description is justified?

Answer and explanation

C. A possible duplicate charge under shift-lead review

The brief supplies a pending query, not a confirmed error, motive, or adjustment.

3. Which ownership arrangement is clear?

Answer and explanation

C. Maya accepts guest follow-up while the shift lead reviews the charge.

The arrangement distinguishes guest communication from the decision authority and gives both work streams an owner.

4. What must precede calling table 5's check closed?

Answer and explanation

A. The actual relevant payment and closure process

A staffing handover does not establish that the bill has been paid or closed.
